People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor... I came in because... well, it's about the domino table. I play down at the center, you know. Been going there since my brother passed back in '98. He was a real good player, my brother. Always held onto his double six too long, though. I tell the fellas now, you can't hold onto it like that, but they don't listen. 

But anyway, the men at the table... lately they've been giving me these little messages. Just little things meant for me. And it's not just them, sometimes the radio announcer does it too. I always listen to the AM station in the mornings while I have my coffee. Used to be a fellow on there who talked about the weather, sounded just like my Uncle Joe. Joe had a farm out east, grew the biggest tomatoes you ever saw... 

But the messages, my wife, she says it's just talk. She tells me to just play my tiles and ignore it. But I know they are warning me to be careful. You just have to be careful these days. Especially with how they changed the bus schedule... I almost missed my ride here because they moved the stop down a whole block, didn't even put up a sign...

Memory? No, doctor, my memory is sharp as a tack. I haven't noticed any trouble with it at all. Why, I can remember things from way back better than these young folks remember yesterday. Like my first car... it was a '54 Ford. Blue, with a white roof. I bought it off a fellow named Miller who lived down by the old creek. 

That creek used to flood every spring, washed out the wooden bridge twice in one decade. They finally put in a concrete one, but it took the county three whole years to get around to it. Three years we had to drive the long way around past the Miller place just to get to town. He had these two big hound dogs, always howling at the moon... kept the whole neighborhood awake. Course, there wasn't much of a neighborhood back then, just dirt roads and pine trees. They cut all those trees down in the seventies to build that strip mall. The one with the hardware store that closed last year. Shame, really, they always had the best prices on nails...

Confused? No, no, my head's as clear as a bell. I don't get confused. Well, maybe just about how they run things down at city hall these days. You know, I went down there last month to pay my water bill. Used to be you could just hand it to Mary at the front desk. She's been there since... oh, must be 1985. We went to the same church, Saint Jude's, over on Elm Street. 

They got a new priest there now, Father Thomas. Nice fellow, but he talks too fast. You can barely understand the homily. Not like Father O'Malley, he used to project his voice so even the folks in the back pew could hear every word. He had this big booming voice, like a foghorn. Speaking of fog, we used to get the thickest fog coming off the river in November. You couldn't even see your hand in front of your face. I remember one time, my buddy Frank and I were trying to walk home from the bowling alley in it... Frank, he bowled a 300 game once. Got his picture in the local paper and everything. But I know they are warning me to be careful. You just have to be careful these days. Especially with how they changed the bus schedule... I almost missed my ride here because they moved the stop down a whole block, didn't even put up a sign...\n\nDoctor: Do you believe for certain that the men at the domino table are sending you messages meant specifically for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, I know it for certain. Yes, sir. Even when my wife says it's just talk... she always says, \"Just play your tiles.\" She's been brushing me off like that since we bought our first house. Cost us eight thousand dollars back then. Eight thousand! And it had a real nice front porch. I used to sit out there in the evenings and whittle. Made a little wooden bird for my grandson once. He’s in college now, studying... well, I don't know what he's studying, something with the computers. Everything is computers now, nobody works with their hands. \n\nBut I know what I hear, doctor. Those men at the table, and the radio announcer too, they give little messages meant just for me. I know they are warning me to be careful. You just have to be careful. Like with the groceries... the prices they charge for a carton of eggs now, it's highway robbery. I went to the store on Tuesday and the girl at the register, she didn't even look up from her phone to give me my change. Just handed it right over without a word...\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory lately?\n\nPatient: Memory? No, doctor, my memory is sharp as a tack. I haven't noticed any trouble with it at all. Why, I can remember things from way back better than these young folks remember yesterday. Like my first car... it was a '54 Ford. Blue, with a white roof. I bought it off a fellow named Miller who lived down by the old creek. \n\nThat creek used to flood every spring, washed out the wooden bridge twice in one decade. They finally put in a concrete one, but it took the county three whole years to get around to it. Three years we had to drive the long way around past the Miller place just to get to town. He had these two big hound dogs, always howling at the moon... kept the whole neighborhood awake. Course, there wasn't much of a neighborhood back then, just dirt roads and pine trees. They cut all those trees down in the seventies to build that strip mall. The one with the hardware store that closed last year. Shame, really, they always had the best prices on nails...\n\nDoctor: Have there been times lately when you felt unusually confused or not fully clear-headed?\n\nPatient: Confused? No, no, my head's as clear as a bell. I don't get confused. Well, maybe just about how they run things down at city hall these days. You know, I went down there last month to pay my water bill. Used to be you could just hand it to Mary at the front desk. She's been there since... oh, must be 1985. We went to the same church, Saint Jude's, over on Elm Street. \n\nThey got a new priest there now, Father Thomas. Nice fellow, but he talks too fast. You can barely understand the homily. Not like Father O'Malley, he used to project his voice so even the folks in the back pew could hear every word. He had this big booming voice, like a foghorn. Speaking of fog, we used to get the thickest fog coming off the river in November. You couldn't even see your hand in front of your face. I remember one time, my buddy Frank and I were trying to walk home from the bowling alley in it... Frank, he bowled a 300 game once. Got his picture in the local paper and everything. They gave him a little trophy with a gold bowler on top...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Lost? Oh, no, sir. I know my way around this town like the back of my hand. I've walked these streets since they were dirt. Never get lost. Although, you know, they did go and change the name of Main Street to Martin Luther King Boulevard a few years back. Caused a whole lot of fuss at the time. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she was furious because she had to get all new return address labels printed. 

She used to order those labels from a catalog... Harriet Carter, I think it was. They sold all sorts of little knick-knacks. I bought a set of those magnetic screwdrivers from them once. Best thing I ever bought. You drop a screw in the engine block, you just fish it right out. I was working on my son's lawnmower when I first used 'em. It was one of those old Snapper riding mowers, the red ones. He left it out in the rain all winter and the carburetor got all gummed up. Kids today, they don't take care of their things. Just throw it away and buy a new one, that's their motto. Like those paper plates... my wife bought a whole stack of them for the Fourth of July barbecue. We always had ribs, slow-cooked over hickory wood...

But I know they are warning me to be careful. You just have to be careful these days. Especially with how they changed the bus schedule... I almost missed my ride here because they moved the stop down a whole block, didn't even put up a sign...\n\nDoctor: Do you believe for certain that the men at the domino table are sending you messages meant specifically for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, I know it for certain. Yes, sir. Even when my wife says it's just talk... she always says, \"Just play your tiles.\" She's been brushing me off like that since we bought our first house. Cost us eight thousand dollars back then. Eight thousand! And it had a real nice front porch. I used to sit out there in the evenings and whittle. Made a little wooden bird for my grandson once. He’s in college now, studying... well, I don't know what he's studying, something with the computers. Everything is computers now, nobody works with their hands. \n\nBut I know what I hear, doctor. Those men at the table, and the radio announcer too, they give little messages meant just for me. I know they are warning me to be careful. You just have to be careful. Like with the groceries... the prices they charge for a carton of eggs now, it's highway robbery. I went to the store on Tuesday and the girl at the register, she didn't even look up from her phone to give me my change. Just handed it right over without a word...\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory lately?\n\nPatient: Memory? No, doctor, my memory is sharp as a tack. I haven't noticed any trouble with it at all. Why, I can remember things from way back better than these young folks remember yesterday. Like my first car... it was a '54 Ford. Blue, with a white roof. I bought it off a fellow named Miller who lived down by the old creek. \n\nThat creek used to flood every spring, washed out the wooden bridge twice in one decade. They finally put in a concrete one, but it took the county three whole years to get around to it. Three years we had to drive the long way around past the Miller place just to get to town. He had these two big hound dogs, always howling at the moon... kept the whole neighborhood awake. Course, there wasn't much of a neighborhood back then, just dirt roads and pine trees. They cut all those trees down in the seventies to build that strip mall. The one with the hardware store that closed last year. Shame, really, they always had the best prices on nails...\n\nDoctor: Have there been times lately when you felt unusually confused or not fully clear-headed?\n\nPatient: Confused? No, no, my head's as clear as a bell. I don't get confused. Well, maybe just about how they run things down at city hall these days. You know, I went down there last month to pay my water bill. Used to be you could just hand it to Mary at the front desk. She's been there since... oh, must be 1985. We went to the same church, Saint Jude's, over on Elm Street. \n\nThey got a new priest there now, Father Thomas. Nice fellow, but he talks too fast. You can barely understand the homily. Not like Father O'Malley, he used to project his voice so even the folks in the back pew could hear every word. He had this big booming voice, like a foghorn. Speaking of fog, we used to get the thickest fog coming off the river in November. You couldn't even see your hand in front of your face. I remember one time, my buddy Frank and I were trying to walk home from the bowling alley in it... Frank, he bowled a 300 game once. Got his picture in the local paper and everything. They gave him a little trophy with a gold bowler on top...\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that should be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: Lost? Oh, no, sir. I know my way around this town like the back of my hand. I've walked these streets since they were dirt. Never get lost. Although, you know, they did go and change the name of Main Street to Martin Luther King Boulevard a few years back. Caused a whole lot of fuss at the time. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she was furious because she had to get all new return address labels printed. \n\nShe used to order those labels from a catalog... Harriet Carter, I think it was. They sold all sorts of little knick-knacks. I bought a set of those magnetic screwdrivers from them once. Best thing I ever bought. You drop a screw in the engine block, you just fish it right out. I was working on my son's lawnmower when I first used 'em. It was one of those old Snapper riding mowers, the red ones. He left it out in the rain all winter and the carburetor got all gummed up. Kids today, they don't take care of their things. Just throw it away and buy a new one, that's their motto. Like those paper plates... my wife bought a whole stack of them for the Fourth of July barbecue. We always had ribs, slow-cooked over hickory wood...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Nobody's told me that. My daughter Betty comes by every Sunday and she's never said a peep about my memory. She usually brings a pot roast. Now, she makes a fair roast, but she puts way too much salt in the gravy. I always tell her she's gonna rust her pipes with all that salt, but she don't listen. Just like my brother Arthur, he used to salt his ham. Can you imagine that, salting a cured ham. He worked down at the railyard for forty years, loading freight. Hard work, that was. He had these big thick leather gloves he wore right through the fingers. Bought them at the old mercantile. You just can't find good leather gloves like that anymore. Everything now is made of plastic or nylon or whatever they call it. Makes your hands sweat something awful when the summer heat sets in...

They finally put in a concrete one, but it took the county three whole years to get around to it. Three years we had to drive the long way around past the Miller place just to get to town. I was working on my son's lawnmower when I first used 'em. It was one of those old Snapper riding mowers, the red ones. He left it out in the rain all winter and the carburetor got all gummed up. Kids today, they don't take care of their things. Just throw it away and buy a new one, that's their motto. Like those paper plates... my wife bought a whole stack of them for the Fourth of July barbecue. We always had ribs, slow-cooked over hickory wood...\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you told you that your memory has been getting worse?\n\nPatient: Nobody's told me that. Trouble with bills? No, no, I pay 'em just fine. Every month, right on time. I keep 'em all in this old cigar box on the kitchen table. It's a wooden box, real sturdy... Dutch Masters, I think. My uncle Charlie used to smoke those. He'd sit on the porch all evening, puffing away, telling stories about the Navy. He was stationed out in Hawaii before the war. Brought back this little ukulele for my sister. She tried to learn how to play it, but she didn't have much of an ear for music. Not like my cousin Sarah, she could play the piano by ear. Just hear a song on the radio once and sit down and play it perfect. We used to listen to the Grand Ole Opry on Saturday nights. Had this big wooden console radio, took up half the living room. The tubes in the back would glow orange when it warmed up...

Not like Father O'Malley, he used to project his voice so even the folks in the back pew could hear every word. He had this big booming voice, like a foghorn. Speaking of fog, we used to get the thickest fog coming off the river in November. You couldn't even see your hand in front of your face. I remember one time, my buddy Frank and I were trying to walk home from the bowling alley in it... Frank, he bowled a 300 game once. Got his picture in the local paper and everything. They gave him a little trophy with a gold bowler on top...\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that should be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: Lost? Oh, no, sir. I know my way around this town like the back of my hand. I've walked these streets since they were dirt. Never get lost. Although, you know, they did go and change the name of Main Street to Martin Luther King Boulevard a few years back. Caused a whole lot of fuss at the time. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she was furious because she had to get all new return address labels printed. \n\nShe used to order those labels from a catalog... Harriet Carter, I think it was. They sold all sorts of little knick-knacks. I bought a set of those magnetic screwdrivers from them once. Best thing I ever bought. You drop a screw in the engine block, you just fish it right out. I was working on my son's lawnmower when I first used 'em. It was one of those old Snapper riding mowers, the red ones. He left it out in the rain all winter and the carburetor got all gummed up. Kids today, they don't take care of their things. Just throw it away and buy a new one, that's their motto. Like those paper plates... my wife bought a whole stack of them for the Fourth of July barbecue. We always had ribs, slow-cooked over hickory wood...\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you told you that your memory has been getting worse?\n\nPatient: Nobody's told me that. My daughter Betty comes by every Sunday and she's never said a peep about my memory. She usually brings a pot roast. Now, she makes a fair roast, but she puts way too much salt in the gravy. I always tell her she's gonna rust her pipes with all that salt, but she don't listen. Just like my brother Arthur, he used to salt his ham. Can you imagine that, salting a cured ham. He worked down at the railyard for forty years, loading freight. Hard work, that was. He had these big thick leather gloves he wore right through the fingers. Bought them at the old mercantile. You just can't find good leather gloves like that anymore. Everything now is made of plastic or nylon or whatever they call it. Makes your hands sweat something awful when the summer heat sets in...\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble managing your money or paying bills lately?\n\nPatient: Trouble with bills? No, no, I pay 'em just fine. Every month, right on time. I keep 'em all in this old cigar box on the kitchen table. It's a wooden box, real sturdy... Dutch Masters, I think. My uncle Charlie used to smoke those. He'd sit on the porch all evening, puffing away, telling stories about the Navy. He was stationed out in Hawaii before the war. Brought back this little ukulele for my sister. She tried to learn how to play it, but she didn't have much of an ear for music. Not like my cousin Sarah, she could play the piano by ear. Just hear a song on the radio once and sit down and play it perfect. We used to listen to the Grand Ole Opry on Saturday nights. Had this big wooden console radio, took up half the living room. The tubes in the back would glow orange when it warmed up...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Voices? Well, you know, sometimes in the afternoons when the house gets real quiet... I'll hear my old friend Jimmy call my name from out on the porch. Or sometimes he's humming one of those old choir songs, clear as day, like he's standing right there next to me. Jimmy had a beautiful baritone voice. We used to sing in the church choir together back in... oh, must have been the fifties. The choir director was a fellow named Mr. Henderson, wore these thick horn-rimmed glasses that always slid down his nose when he got worked up waving his arms around. He drove a Nash Ambassador, one of those big heavy cars that looked like an upside-down bathtub. Smooth ride, though. We took it all the way to the state fair one year, packed five of us in there with a picnic basket my mother made. She fried up a whole chicken in a cast iron skillet. You can't get chicken that tastes like that anymore, all the birds today are pumped full of water and raised in those big metal sheds...

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F20.0, F06.2, F03\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor... I came in because... well, it's about the domino table. I play down at the center, you know. Been going there since my brother passed back in '98. He was a real good player, my brother. Always held onto his double six too long, though. I tell the fellas now, you can't hold onto it like that, but they don't listen. \n\nBut anyway, the men at the table... lately they've been giving me these little messages. Just little things meant for me. And it's not just them, sometimes the radio announcer does it too. I always listen to the AM station in the mornings while I have my coffee. Used to be a fellow on there who talked about the weather, sounded just like my Uncle Joe. Joe had a farm out east, grew the biggest tomatoes you ever saw... \n\nBut the messages, my wife, she says it's just talk. She tells me to just play my tiles and ignore it. But I know they are warning me to be careful. You just have to be careful these days. Especially with how they changed the bus schedule... I almost missed my ride here because they moved the stop down a whole block, didn't even put up a sign...\n\nDoctor: Do you believe for certain that the men at the domino table are sending you messages meant specifically for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, I know it for certain. Yes, sir. Even when my wife says it's just talk... she always says, \"Just play your tiles.\" She's been brushing me off like that since we bought our first house. Cost us eight thousand dollars back then. Eight thousand! And it had a real nice front porch. I used to sit out there in the evenings and whittle. Made a little wooden bird for my grandson once. He’s in college now, studying... well, I don't know what he's studying, something with the computers. Everything is computers now, nobody works with their hands. \n\nBut I know what I hear, doctor. Those men at the table, and the radio announcer too, they give little messages meant just for me. I know they are warning me to be careful. You just have to be careful. Like with the groceries... the prices they charge for a carton of eggs now, it's highway robbery. I went to the store on Tuesday and the girl at the register, she didn't even look up from her phone to give me my change. Just handed it right over without a word...\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory lately?\n\nPatient: Memory? No, doctor, my memory is sharp as a tack. I haven't noticed any trouble with it at all. Why, I can remember things from way back better than these young folks remember yesterday. Like my first car... it was a '54 Ford. Blue, with a white roof. I bought it off a fellow named Miller who lived down by the old creek. \n\nThat creek used to flood every spring, washed out the wooden bridge twice in one decade. They finally put in a concrete one, but it took the county three whole years to get around to it. Three years we had to drive the long way around past the Miller place just to get to town. He had these two big hound dogs, always howling at the moon... kept the whole neighborhood awake. Course, there wasn't much of a neighborhood back then, just dirt roads and pine trees. They cut all those trees down in the seventies to build that strip mall. The one with the hardware store that closed last year. Shame, really, they always had the best prices on nails...\n\nDoctor: Have there been times lately when you felt unusually confused or not fully clear-headed?\n\nPatient: Confused? No, no, my head's as clear as a bell. I don't get confused. Well, maybe just about how they run things down at city hall these days. You know, I went down there last month to pay my water bill. Used to be you could just hand it to Mary at the front desk. She's been there since... oh, must be 1985. We went to the same church, Saint Jude's, over on Elm Street. \n\nThey got a new priest there now, Father Thomas. Nice fellow, but he talks too fast. You can barely understand the homily. Not like Father O'Malley, he used to project his voice so even the folks in the back pew could hear every word. He had this big booming voice, like a foghorn. Speaking of fog, we used to get the thickest fog coming off the river in November. You couldn't even see your hand in front of your face. I remember one time, my buddy Frank and I were trying to walk home from the bowling alley in it... Frank, he bowled a 300 game once. Got his picture in the local paper and everything. They gave him a little trophy with a gold bowler on top...\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that should be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: Lost? Oh, no, sir. I know my way around this town like the back of my hand. I've walked these streets since they were dirt. Never get lost. Although, you know, they did go and change the name of Main Street to Martin Luther King Boulevard a few years back. Caused a whole lot of fuss at the time. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she was furious because she had to get all new return address labels printed. \n\nShe used to order those labels from a catalog... Harriet Carter, I think it was. They sold all sorts of little knick-knacks. I bought a set of those magnetic screwdrivers from them once. Best thing I ever bought. You drop a screw in the engine block, you just fish it right out. I was working on my son's lawnmower when I first used 'em. It was one of those old Snapper riding mowers, the red ones. He left it out in the rain all winter and the carburetor got all gummed up. Kids today, they don't take care of their things. Just throw it away and buy a new one, that's their motto. Like those paper plates... my wife bought a whole stack of them for the Fourth of July barbecue. We always had ribs, slow-cooked over hickory wood...\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you told you that your memory has been getting worse?\n\nPatient: Nobody's told me that. My daughter Betty comes by every Sunday and she's never said a peep about my memory. She usually brings a pot roast. Now, she makes a fair roast, but she puts way too much salt in the gravy. I always tell her she's gonna rust her pipes with all that salt, but she don't listen. Just like my brother Arthur, he used to salt his ham. Can you imagine that, salting a cured ham. He worked down at the railyard for forty years, loading freight. Hard work, that was. He had these big thick leather gloves he wore right through the fingers. Bought them at the old mercantile. You just can't find good leather gloves like that anymore. Everything now is made of plastic or nylon or whatever they call it. Makes your hands sweat something awful when the summer heat sets in...\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble managing your money or paying bills lately?\n\nPatient: Trouble with bills? No, no, I pay 'em just fine. Every month, right on time. I keep 'em all in this old cigar box on the kitchen table. It's a wooden box, real sturdy... Dutch Masters, I think. My uncle Charlie used to smoke those. He'd sit on the porch all evening, puffing away, telling stories about the Navy. He was stationed out in Hawaii before the war. Brought back this little ukulele for my sister. She tried to learn how to play it, but she didn't have much of an ear for music. Not like my cousin Sarah, she could play the piano by ear. Just hear a song on the radio once and sit down and play it perfect. We used to listen to the Grand Ole Opry on Saturday nights. Had this big wooden console radio, took up half the living room. The tubes in the back would glow orange when it warmed up...\n\nDoctor: Have you heard voices when no one else was there?\n\nPatient: Voices? Well, you know, sometimes in the afternoons when the house gets real quiet... I'll hear my old friend Jimmy call my name from out on the porch. Or sometimes he's humming one of those old choir songs, clear as day, like he's standing right there next to me. Jimmy had a beautiful baritone voice. We used to sing in the church choir together back in... oh, must have been the fifties. The choir director was a fellow named Mr. Henderson, wore these thick horn-rimmed glasses that always slid down his nose when he got worked up waving his arms around. He drove a Nash Ambassador, one of those big heavy cars that looked like an upside-down bathtub. Smooth ride, though. We took it all the way to the state fair one year, packed five of us in there with a picnic basket my mother made. She fried up a whole chicken in a cast iron skillet. You can't get chicken that tastes like that anymore, all the birds today are pumped full of water and raised in those big metal sheds...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Makes your hands sweat something awful when the summer heat sets in...\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble managing your money or paying bills lately?\n\nPatient: Trouble with bills? No, no, I pay 'em just fine. Every month, right on time. I keep 'em all in this old cigar box on the kitchen table. It's a wooden box, real sturdy... Dutch Masters, I think. My uncle Charlie used to smoke those. He'd sit on the porch all evening, puffing away, telling stories about the Navy. He was stationed out in Hawaii before the war. Brought back this little ukulele for my sister. She tried to learn how to play it, but she didn't have much of an ear for music. Not like my cousin Sarah, she could play the piano by ear. Just hear a song on the radio once and sit down and play it perfect. We used to listen to the Grand Ole Opry on Saturday nights. Had this big wooden console radio, took up half the living room. The tubes in the back would glow orange when it warmed up...\n\nDoctor: Have you heard voices when no one else was there?\n\nPatient: Voices? Well, you know, sometimes in the afternoons when the house gets real quiet... I'll hear my old friend Jimmy call my name from out on the porch. Or sometimes he's humming one of those old choir songs, clear as day, like he's standing right there next to me. Jimmy had a beautiful baritone voice. We used to sing in the church choir together back in... oh, must have been the fifties. The choir director was a fellow named Mr. Henderson, wore these thick horn-rimmed glasses that always slid down his nose when he got worked up waving his arms around. He drove a Nash Ambassador, one of those big heavy cars that looked like an upside-down bathtub. Smooth ride, though. We took it all the way to the state fair one year, packed five of us in there with a picnic basket my mother made. She fried up a whole chicken in a cast iron skillet. You can't get chicken that tastes like that anymore, all the birds today are pumped full of water and raised in those big metal sheds...\n\n[Last Question]\nIs Jimmy still alive?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 84 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about 1 year and 5 months, since after my close friend passed away.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, Jimmy... no, he passed away. It's been about a year and five months now since we lost him. That's right around when this all started, hearing him and all, right after he died. We laid him to rest out at Oak Hill Cemetery. They got him a real nice spot, right under this massive weeping willow tree. I always did like willow trees, the way they just sort of sway when the breeze catches them. My grandfather had a big old willow in his front yard back in Ohio. We used to swing from the branches when we were little. You gotta be careful, though, the wood on those branches is mighty soft. It snaps real easy if you put too much weight on it. I fell out of it once, oh, I must have been seven or eight, and scraped my knee up so bad my mother had to paint it with iodine. Boy, did that sting. It was bright orange, left a terrible stain on my good trousers. Getting stains out of clothes back then was a real chore, we didn't have these fancy washing machines with all the buttons and dials. Just a washboard and a big tin tub of hot soapy water...

Just handed it right over without a word...\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory lately?\n\nPatient: Memory? No, doctor, my memory is sharp as a tack. I haven't noticed any trouble with it at all. Why, I can remember things from way back better than these young folks remember yesterday. Like my first car... it was a '54 Ford. Blue, with a white roof. I bought it off a fellow named Miller who lived down by the old creek. \n\nThat creek used to flood every spring, washed out the wooden bridge twice in one decade. They finally put in a concrete one, but it took the county three whole years to get around to it. Three years we had to drive the long way around past the Miller place just to get to town. He had these two big hound dogs, always howling at the moon... kept the whole neighborhood awake. Course, there wasn't much of a neighborhood back then, just dirt roads and pine trees. They cut all those trees down in the seventies to build that strip mall. The one with the hardware store that closed last year. Shame, really, they always had the best prices on nails...\n\nDoctor: Have there been times lately when you felt unusually confused or not fully clear-headed?\n\nPatient: Confused? No, no, my head's as clear as a bell. I don't get confused. Well, maybe just about how they run things down at city hall these days. You know, I went down there last month to pay my water bill. Used to be you could just hand it to Mary at the front desk. She's been there since... oh, must be 1985. We went to the same church, Saint Jude's, over on Elm Street. \n\nThey got a new priest there now, Father Thomas. Nice fellow, but he talks too fast. You can barely understand the homily. Not like Father O'Malley, he used to project his voice so even the folks in the back pew could hear every word. He had this big booming voice, like a foghorn. Speaking of fog, we used to get the thickest fog coming off the river in November. You couldn't even see your hand in front of your face. I remember one time, my buddy Frank and I were trying to walk home from the bowling alley in it... Frank, he bowled a 300 game once. Got his picture in the local paper and everything. They gave him a little trophy with a gold bowler on top...\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that should be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: Lost? Oh, no, sir. They sold all sorts of little knick-knacks. I bought a set of those magnetic screwdrivers from them once. Best thing I ever bought. You drop a screw in the engine block, you just fish it right out. I was working on my son's lawnmower when I first used 'em. It was one of those old Snapper riding mowers, the red ones. He left it out in the rain all winter and the carburetor got all gummed up. Kids today, they don't take care of their things. Just throw it away and buy a new one, that's their motto. Like those paper plates... my wife bought a whole stack of them for the Fourth of July barbecue. We always had ribs, slow-cooked over hickory wood...\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you told you that your memory has been getting worse?\n\nPatient: Nobody's told me that. description: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.\n  manifestation: I still love my family, but many days I just sit stimuli.\n  manifestation: If I am trying to read the church paper or talk with my wife, a car outside, a bird, or a sound from the radio pulls me away and I forget what I was doing.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and manic_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: During my bright, restless weeks when I am sleeping little and making big plans, I still hear my friend’s voice and feel the radio or domino men are sending me warnings.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: manic_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness\n  manifestation: Since all this started, more months have been taken up by those high-energy spells and their aftereffects than by my calmer stretches.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode\n  manifestation: There was a quiet month when I was sleeping normally and not full of big plans, but I still heard my friend on the porch and believed the radio was speaking to me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I have missed choir rehearsals, let parts of my garden go, and made the domino games uncomfortable enough that I do not join like I used to.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 5 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about 1 year and 5 months, since after my close friend passed away.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 9 months\n  manifestation: The voices and special messages have been happening on and off for about 9 months now.\n- key: manic_episode_duration\n  name: Manic Episode Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: Those weeks of high energy, little sleep, and big plans have been coming back for about 11 months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 84 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S019\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble managing your money or paying bills lately?\n\nPatient: Trouble with bills? No, no, I pay 'em just fine. Every month, right on time. I keep 'em all in this old cigar box on the kitchen table. It's a wooden box, real sturdy... Dutch Masters, I think. My uncle Charlie used to smoke those. He'd sit on the porch all evening, puffing away, telling stories about the Navy. He was stationed out in Hawaii before the war. Brought back this little ukulele for my sister. She tried to learn how to play it, but she didn't have much of an ear for music. Not like my cousin Sarah, she could play the piano by ear. Just hear a song on the radio once and sit down and play it perfect. We used to listen to the Grand Ole Opry on Saturday nights. Had this big wooden console radio, took up half the living room. The tubes in the back would glow orange when it warmed up...\n\nDoctor: Have you heard voices when no one else was there?\n\nPatient: Voices? Well, you know, sometimes in the afternoons when the house gets real quiet... I'll hear my old friend Jimmy call my name from out on the porch. Or sometimes he's humming one of those old choir songs, clear as day, like he's standing right there next to me. Jimmy had a beautiful baritone voice. We used to sing in the church choir together back in... oh, must have been the fifties. The choir director was a fellow named Mr. Henderson, wore these thick horn-rimmed glasses that always slid down his nose when he got worked up waving his arms around. He drove a Nash Ambassador, one of those big heavy cars that looked like an upside-down bathtub. Smooth ride, though. We took it all the way to the state fair one year, packed five of us in there with a picnic basket my mother made. She fried up a whole chicken in a cast iron skillet. You can't get chicken that tastes like that anymore, all the birds today are pumped full of water and raised in those big metal sheds...\n\nDoctor: Is Jimmy still alive?\n\nPatient: No, Jimmy... no, he passed away. It's been about a year and five months now since we lost him. That's right around when this all started, hearing him and all, right after he died. We laid him to rest out at Oak Hill Cemetery. They got him a real nice spot, right under this massive weeping willow tree. I always did like willow trees, the way they just sort of sway when the breeze catches them. My grandfather had a big old willow in his front yard back in Ohio. We used to swing from the branches when we were little. You gotta be careful, though, the wood on those branches is mighty soft. It snaps real easy if you put too much weight on it. I fell out of it once, oh, I must have been seven or eight, and scraped my knee up so bad my mother had to paint it with iodine. Boy, did that sting. It was bright orange, left a terrible stain on my good trousers. Getting stains out of clothes back then was a real chore, we didn't have these fancy washing machines with all the buttons and dials. Just a washboard and a big tin tub of hot soapy water...\n\n[Last Question]\nHas your wife said that you’ve been more forgetful lately?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 10\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Forgetful? No, Martha hasn't said anything to me about that. I remember things just fine. Now, Martha, she's the one who writes everything down on those little yellow pads. Leaves 'em stuck to the icebox door so she doesn't forget to buy milk or butter. We just got a new icebox last year, one of those shiny silver ones. Terrible thing to keep clean, shows every single fingerprint the second you touch it. I told her we should have kept the old white one, it ran just fine. It was a Whirlpool, bought it over at Sears Roebuck back when they had the big store downtown. You could buy anything at Sears back then, tools, clothes, even a whole house out of a catalog. My old neighbor, Mr. Higgins, his father built one of those catalog houses. Came on a train in a hundred different boxes, every piece of lumber stamped with a number. Took him and his brothers a whole summer to put it together. They used real solid oak for the floors, not like the cheap stuff they build with today. You walk into a new house now and the floors just squeak and bounce right under your boots...

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F20.0, F06.2, F03\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor... I came in because... well, it's about the domino table. I play down at the center, you know. Been going there since my brother passed back in '98. He was a real good player, my brother. Always held onto his double six too long, though. I tell the fellas now, you can't hold onto it like that, but they don't listen. \n\nBut anyway, the men at the table... lately they've been giving me these little messages. Just little things meant for me. And it's not just them, sometimes the radio announcer does it too. I always listen to the AM station in the mornings while I have my coffee. Used to be a fellow on there who talked about the weather, sounded just like my Uncle Joe. Joe had a farm out east, grew the biggest tomatoes you ever saw... \n\nBut the messages, my wife, she says it's just talk. She tells me to just play my tiles and ignore it. But I know they are warning me to be careful. You just have to be careful these days. Especially with how they changed the bus schedule... I almost missed my ride here because they moved the stop down a whole block, didn't even put up a sign...\n\nDoctor: Do you believe for certain that the men at the domino table are sending you messages meant specifically for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, I know it for certain. Yes, sir. Even when my wife says it's just talk... she always says, \"Just play your tiles.\" She's been brushing me off like that since we bought our first house. Cost us eight thousand dollars back then. Eight thousand! And it had a real nice front porch. I used to sit out there in the evenings and whittle. Made a little wooden bird for my grandson once. He’s in college now, studying... well, I don't know what he's studying, something with the computers. Everything is computers now, nobody works with their hands. \n\nBut I know what I hear, doctor. Those men at the table, and the radio announcer too, they give little messages meant just for me. I know they are warning me to be careful. You just have to be careful. Like with the groceries... the prices they charge for a carton of eggs now, it's highway robbery. I went to the store on Tuesday and the girl at the register, she didn't even look up from her phone to give me my change. Just handed it right over without a word...\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory lately?\n\nPatient: Memory? No, doctor, my memory is sharp as a tack. I haven't noticed any trouble with it at all. Why, I can remember things from way back better than these young folks remember yesterday. Like my first car... it was a '54 Ford. Blue, with a white roof. I bought it off a fellow named Miller who lived down by the old creek. \n\nThat creek used to flood every spring, washed out the wooden bridge twice in one decade. They finally put in a concrete one, but it took the county three whole years to get around to it. Three years we had to drive the long way around past the Miller place just to get to town. He had these two big hound dogs, always howling at the moon... kept the whole neighborhood awake. Course, there wasn't much of a neighborhood back then, just dirt roads and pine trees. They cut all those trees down in the seventies to build that strip mall. The one with the hardware store that closed last year. Shame, really, they always had the best prices on nails...\n\nDoctor: Have there been times lately when you felt unusually confused or not fully clear-headed?\n\nPatient: Confused? No, no, my head's as clear as a bell. I don't get confused. Well, maybe just about how they run things down at city hall these days. You know, I went down there last month to pay my water bill. Used to be you could just hand it to Mary at the front desk. She's been there since... oh, must be 1985. We went to the same church, Saint Jude's, over on Elm Street. \n\nThey got a new priest there now, Father Thomas. Nice fellow, but he talks too fast. You can barely understand the homily. Not like Father O'Malley, he used to project his voice so even the folks in the back pew could hear every word. He had this big booming voice, like a foghorn. Speaking of fog, we used to get the thickest fog coming off the river in November. You couldn't even see your hand in front of your face. I remember one time, my buddy Frank and I were trying to walk home from the bowling alley in it... Frank, he bowled a 300 game once. Got his picture in the local paper and everything. They gave him a little trophy with a gold bowler on top...\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that should be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: Lost? Oh, no, sir. I know my way around this town like the back of my hand. I've walked these streets since they were dirt. Never get lost. Although, you know, they did go and change the name of Main Street to Martin Luther King Boulevard a few years back. Caused a whole lot of fuss at the time. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she was furious because she had to get all new return address labels printed. \n\nShe used to order those labels from a catalog... Harriet Carter, I think it was. They sold all sorts of little knick-knacks. I bought a set of those magnetic screwdrivers from them once. Best thing I ever bought. You drop a screw in the engine block, you just fish it right out. I was working on my son's lawnmower when I first used 'em. It was one of those old Snapper riding mowers, the red ones. He left it out in the rain all winter and the carburetor got all gummed up. Kids today, they don't take care of their things. Just throw it away and buy a new one, that's their motto. Like those paper plates... my wife bought a whole stack of them for the Fourth of July barbecue. We always had ribs, slow-cooked over hickory wood...\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you told you that your memory has been getting worse?\n\nPatient: Nobody's told me that. My daughter Betty comes by every Sunday and she's never said a peep about my memory. She usually brings a pot roast. Now, she makes a fair roast, but she puts way too much salt in the gravy. I always tell her she's gonna rust her pipes with all that salt, but she don't listen. Just like my brother Arthur, he used to salt his ham. Can you imagine that, salting a cured ham. He worked down at the railyard for forty years, loading freight. Hard work, that was. He had these big thick leather gloves he wore right through the fingers. Bought them at the old mercantile. You just can't find good leather gloves like that anymore. Everything now is made of plastic or nylon or whatever they call it. Makes your hands sweat something awful when the summer heat sets in...\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble managing your money or paying bills lately?\n\nPatient: Trouble with bills? No, no, I pay 'em just fine. Every month, right on time. I keep 'em all in this old cigar box on the kitchen table. It's a wooden box, real sturdy... Dutch Masters, I think. My uncle Charlie used to smoke those. He'd sit on the porch all evening, puffing away, telling stories about the Navy. He was stationed out in Hawaii before the war. Brought back this little ukulele for my sister. She tried to learn how to play it, but she didn't have much of an ear for music. Not like my cousin Sarah, she could play the piano by ear. Just hear a song on the radio once and sit down and play it perfect. We used to listen to the Grand Ole Opry on Saturday nights. Had this big wooden console radio, took up half the living room. The tubes in the back would glow orange when it warmed up...\n\nDoctor: Have you heard voices when no one else was there?\n\nPatient: Voices? They sold all sorts of little knick-knacks. I bought a set of those magnetic screwdrivers from them once. Best thing I ever bought. You drop a screw in the engine block, you just fish it right out. I was working on my son's lawnmower when I first used 'em. It was one of those old Snapper riding mowers, the red ones. He left it out in the rain all winter and the carburetor got all gummed up. Kids today, they don't take care of their things. Just throw it away and buy a new one, that's their motto. Like those paper plates... my wife bought a whole stack of them for the Fourth of July barbecue. We always had ribs, slow-cooked over hickory wood...\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you told you that your memory has been getting worse?\n\nPatient: Nobody's told me that. My daughter Betty comes by every Sunday and she's never said a peep about my memory. She usually brings a pot roast. Now, she makes a fair roast, but she puts way too much salt in the gravy. I always tell her she's gonna rust her pipes with all that salt, but she don't listen. Just like my brother Arthur, he used to salt his ham. Can you imagine that, salting a cured ham. He worked down at the railyard for forty years, loading freight. Hard work, that was. He had these big thick leather gloves he wore right through the fingers. Bought them at the old mercantile. You just can't find good leather gloves like that anymore. Everything now is made of plastic or nylon or whatever they call it. Makes your hands sweat something awful when the summer heat sets in...\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble managing your money or paying bills lately?\n\nPatient: Trouble with bills? No, no, I pay 'em just fine. Every month, right on time. I keep 'em all in this old cigar box on the kitchen table. It's a wooden box, real sturdy... Dutch Masters, I think. My uncle Charlie used to smoke those. He'd sit on the porch all evening, puffing away, telling stories about the Navy. He was stationed out in Hawaii before the war. Brought back this little ukulele for my sister. She tried to learn how to play it, but she didn't have much of an ear for music. Not like my cousin Sarah, she could play the piano by ear. Just hear a song on the radio once and sit down and play it perfect. We used to listen to the Grand Ole Opry on Saturday nights. Had this big wooden console radio, took up half the living room. The tubes in the back would glow orange when it warmed up...\n\nDoctor: Have you heard voices when no one else was there?\n\nPatient: Voices? Well, you know, sometimes in the afternoons when the house gets real quiet... I'll hear my old friend Jimmy call my name from out on the porch. Or sometimes he's humming one of those old choir songs, clear as day, like he's standing right there next to me. Jimmy had a beautiful baritone voice. We used to sing in the church choir together back in... oh, must have been the fifties. The choir director was a fellow named Mr. Henderson, wore these thick horn-rimmed glasses that always slid down his nose when he got worked up waving his arms around. He drove a Nash Ambassador, one of those big heavy cars that looked like an upside-down bathtub. Smooth ride, though. We took it all the way to the state fair one year, packed five of us in there with a picnic basket my mother made. She fried up a whole chicken in a cast iron skillet. You can't get chicken that tastes like that anymore, all the birds today are pumped full of water and raised in those big metal sheds...\n\nDoctor: Is Jimmy still alive?\n\nPatient: No, Jimmy... no, he passed away. It's been about a year and five months now since we lost him. That's right around when this all started, hearing him and all, right after he died. We laid him to rest out at Oak Hill Cemetery. They got him a real nice spot, right under this massive weeping willow tree. I always did like willow trees, the way they just sort of sway when the breeze catches them. My grandfather had a big old willow in his front yard back in Ohio. We used to swing from the branches when we were little. You gotta be careful, though, the wood on those branches is mighty soft. It snaps real easy if you put too much weight on it. I fell out of it once, oh, I must have been seven or eight, and scraped my knee up so bad my mother had to paint it with iodine. Boy, did that sting. It was bright orange, left a terrible stain on my good trousers. Getting stains out of clothes back then was a real chore, we didn't have these fancy washing machines with all the buttons and dials. Just a washboard and a big tin tub of hot soapy water...\n\nDoctor: Has your wife said that you’ve been more forgetful lately?\n\nPatient: Forgetful? No, Martha hasn't said anything to me about that. I remember things just fine. Now, Martha, she's the one who writes everything down on those little yellow pads. Leaves 'em stuck to the icebox door so she doesn't forget to buy milk or butter. We just got a new icebox last year, one of those shiny silver ones. Terrible thing to keep clean, shows every single fingerprint the second you touch it. I told her we should have kept the old white one, it ran just fine. It was a Whirlpool, bought it over at Sears Roebuck back when they had the big store downtown. You could buy anything at Sears back then, tools, clothes, even a whole house out of a catalog. My old neighbor, Mr. Higgins, his father built one of those catalog houses. Came on a train in a hundred different boxes, every piece of lumber stamped with a number. Took him and his brothers a whole summer to put it together. They used real solid oak for the floors, not like the cheap stuff they build with today. You walk into a new house now and the floors just squeak and bounce right under your boots...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Depressed? No, I wouldn't say that. I feel alright, mostly. Just... you know, regular. Though the weather hasn't been helping much lately, has it? All this rain we've been getting, it just turns the yard into a muddy mess. Martha's been after me to reseed the back lawn, but you can't put down seed when the ground is soaking wet like a sponge. It just rots right there in the dirt. I used to keep a beautiful lawn, back when I had my old push mower. The heavy kind, with the spinning blades that you had to sharpen with a file. You don't see those much anymore. Now everyone's got these loud gas-powered things that sound like airplanes taking off on a Sunday morning. Wakes up the whole neighborhood. My neighbor two doors down, he starts his up right at seven o'clock. Guy works at the hardware store, sells paint, I think. Paint is so expensive nowadays, too. I went in there last month just looking for a little quart of white for the porch railing, and they wanted an arm and a leg for it...

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F20.0, F06.2, F03\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor... I came in because... well, it's about the domino table. I play down at the center, you know. Been going there since my brother passed back in '98. He was a real good player, my brother. Always held onto his double six too long, though. I tell the fellas now, you can't hold onto it like that, but they don't listen. \n\nBut anyway, the men at the table... lately they've been giving me these little messages. Just little things meant for me. And it's not just them, sometimes the radio announcer does it too. I always listen to the AM station in the mornings while I have my coffee. Used to be a fellow on there who talked about the weather, sounded just like my Uncle Joe. Joe had a farm out east, grew the biggest tomatoes you ever saw... \n\nBut the messages, my wife, she says it's just talk. She tells me to just play my tiles and ignore it. But I know they are warning me to be careful. You just have to be careful these days. Especially with how they changed the bus schedule... I almost missed my ride here because they moved the stop down a whole block, didn't even put up a sign...\n\nDoctor: Do you believe for certain that the men at the domino table are sending you messages meant specifically for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, I know it for certain. Yes, sir. Even when my wife says it's just talk... she always says, \"Just play your tiles.\" She's been brushing me off like that since we bought our first house. Cost us eight thousand dollars back then. Eight thousand! And it had a real nice front porch. I used to sit out there in the evenings and whittle. Made a little wooden bird for my grandson once. He’s in college now, studying... well, I don't know what he's studying, something with the computers. Everything is computers now, nobody works with their hands. \n\nBut I know what I hear, doctor. Those men at the table, and the radio announcer too, they give little messages meant just for me. I know they are warning me to be careful. You just have to be careful. Like with the groceries... the prices they charge for a carton of eggs now, it's highway robbery. I went to the store on Tuesday and the girl at the register, she didn't even look up from her phone to give me my change. Just handed it right over without a word...\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory lately?\n\nPatient: Memory? No, doctor, my memory is sharp as a tack. I haven't noticed any trouble with it at all. Why, I can remember things from way back better than these young folks remember yesterday. Like my first car... it was a '54 Ford. Blue, with a white roof. I bought it off a fellow named Miller who lived down by the old creek. \n\nThat creek used to flood every spring, washed out the wooden bridge twice in one decade. They finally put in a concrete one, but it took the county three whole years to get around to it. Three years we had to drive the long way around past the Miller place just to get to town. He had these two big hound dogs, always howling at the moon... kept the whole neighborhood awake. Course, there wasn't much of a neighborhood back then, just dirt roads and pine trees. They cut all those trees down in the seventies to build that strip mall. The one with the hardware store that closed last year. Shame, really, they always had the best prices on nails...\n\nDoctor: Have there been times lately when you felt unusually confused or not fully clear-headed?\n\nPatient: Confused? No, no, my head's as clear as a bell. I don't get confused. Well, maybe just about how they run things down at city hall these days. You know, I went down there last month to pay my water bill. Used to be you could just hand it to Mary at the front desk. She's been there since... oh, must be 1985. We went to the same church, Saint Jude's, over on Elm Street. \n\nThey got a new priest there now, Father Thomas. Nice fellow, but he talks too fast. You can barely understand the homily. Not like Father O'Malley, he used to project his voice so even the folks in the back pew could hear every word. He had this big booming voice, like a foghorn. Speaking of fog, we used to get the thickest fog coming off the river in November. You couldn't even see your hand in front of your face. I remember one time, my buddy Frank and I were trying to walk home from the bowling alley in it... Frank, he bowled a 300 game once. Got his picture in the local paper and everything. They gave him a little trophy with a gold bowler on top...\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that should be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: Lost? Oh, no, sir. I know my way around this town like the back of my hand. I've walked these streets since they were dirt. Never get lost. Although, you know, they did go and change the name of Main Street to Martin Luther King Boulevard a few years back. Caused a whole lot of fuss at the time. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she was furious because she had to get all new return address labels printed. \n\nShe used to order those labels from a catalog... Harriet Carter, I think it was. They sold all sorts of little knick-knacks. I bought a set of those magnetic screwdrivers from them once. Best thing I ever bought. You drop a screw in the engine block, you just fish it right out. I was working on my son's lawnmower when I first used 'em. It was one of those old Snapper riding mowers, the red ones. He left it out in the rain all winter and the carburetor got all gummed up. Kids today, they don't take care of their things. Just throw it away and buy a new one, that's their motto. Like those paper plates... my wife bought a whole stack of them for the Fourth of July barbecue. We always had ribs, slow-cooked over hickory wood...\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you told you that your memory has been getting worse?\n\nPatient: Nobody's told me that. My daughter Betty comes by every Sunday and she's never said a peep about my memory. She usually brings a pot roast. Now, she makes a fair roast, but she puts way too much salt in the gravy. I always tell her she's gonna rust her pipes with all that salt, but she don't listen. Just like my brother Arthur, he used to salt his ham. Can you imagine that, salting a cured ham. He worked down at the railyard for forty years, loading freight. Hard work, that was. He had these big thick leather gloves he wore right through the fingers. Bought them at the old mercantile. You just can't find good leather gloves like that anymore. Everything now is made of plastic or nylon or whatever they call it. Makes your hands sweat something awful when the summer heat sets in...\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble managing your money or paying bills lately?\n\nPatient: Trouble with bills? No, no, I pay 'em just fine. Every month, right on time. I keep 'em all in this old cigar box on the kitchen table. It's a wooden box, real sturdy... Dutch Masters, I think. My uncle Charlie used to smoke those. He'd sit on the porch all evening, puffing away, telling stories about the Navy. He was stationed out in Hawaii before the war. Brought back this little ukulele for my sister. She tried to learn how to play it, but she didn't have much of an ear for music. Not like my cousin Sarah, she could play the piano by ear. Just hear a song on the radio once and sit down and play it perfect. We used to listen to the Grand Ole Opry on Saturday nights. Had this big wooden console radio, took up half the living room. The tubes in the back would glow orange when it warmed up...\n\nDoctor: Have you heard voices when no one else was there?\n\nPatient: Voices? Well, you know, sometimes in the afternoons when the house gets real quiet... I'll hear my old friend Jimmy call my name from out on the porch. Or sometimes he's humming one of those old choir songs, clear as day, like he's standing right there next to me. Jimmy had a beautiful baritone voice. We used to sing in the church choir together back in... oh, must have been the fifties. The choir director was a fellow named Mr. Henderson, wore these thick horn-rimmed glasses that always slid down his nose when he got worked up waving his arms around. He drove a Nash Ambassador, one of those big heavy cars that looked like an upside-down bathtub. Smooth ride, though. We took it all the way to the state fair one year, packed five of us in there with a picnic basket my mother made. She fried up a whole chicken in a cast iron skillet. You can't get chicken that tastes like that anymore, all the birds today are pumped full of water and raised in those big metal sheds...\n\nDoctor: Is Jimmy still alive?\n\nPatient: No, Jimmy... no, he passed away. It's been about a year and five months now since we lost him. That's right around when this all started, hearing him and all, right after he died. We laid him to rest out at Oak Hill Cemetery. They got him a real nice spot, right under this massive weeping willow tree. I always did like willow trees, the way they just sort of sway when the breeze catches them. My grandfather had a big old willow in his front yard back in Ohio. We used to swing from the branches when we were little. You gotta be careful, though, the wood on those branches is mighty soft. It snaps real easy if you put too much weight on it. I fell out of it once, oh, I must have been seven or eight, and scraped my knee up so bad my mother had to paint it with iodine. Boy, did that sting. It was bright orange, left a terrible stain on my good trousers. Getting stains out of clothes back then was a real chore, we didn't have these fancy washing machines with all the buttons and dials. Just a washboard and a big tin tub of hot soapy water...\n\nDoctor: Has your wife said that you’ve been more forgetful lately?\n\nPatient: Forgetful? No, Martha hasn't said anything to me about that. I remember things just fine. Now, Martha, she's the one who writes everything down on those little yellow pads. Leaves 'em stuck to the icebox door so she doesn't forget to buy milk or butter. We just got a new icebox last year, one of those shiny silver ones. Terrible thing to keep clean, shows every single fingerprint the second you touch it. I told her we should have kept the old white one, it ran just fine. It was a Whirlpool, bought it over at Sears Roebuck back when they had the big store downtown. You could buy anything at Sears back then, tools, clothes, even a whole house out of a catalog. My old neighbor, Mr. Higgins, his father built one of those catalog houses. Came on a train in a hundred different boxes, every piece of lumber stamped with a number. Took him and his brothers a whole summer to put it together. They used real solid oak for the floors, not like the cheap stuff they build with today. You walk into a new house now and the floors just squeak and bounce right under your boots...\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Depressed? No, I wouldn't say that. I feel alright, mostly. Just... you know, regular. Though the weather hasn't been helping much lately, has it? All this rain we've been getting, it just turns the yard into a muddy mess. Martha's been after me to reseed the back lawn, but you can't put down seed when the ground is soaking wet like a sponge. It just rots right there in the dirt. I used to keep a beautiful lawn, back when I had my old push mower. The heavy kind, with the spinning blades that you had to sharpen with a file. You don't see those much anymore. Now everyone's got these loud gas-powered things that sound like airplanes taking off on a Sunday morning. Wakes up the whole neighborhood. My neighbor two doors down, he starts his up right at seven o'clock. Guy works at the hardware store, sells paint, I think. Paint is so expensive nowadays, too. I went in there last month just looking for a little quart of white for the porch railing, and they wanted an arm and a leg for it...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
