========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F20.0, F06.2, F03\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor... I came in because... well, it's about the domino table. I play down at the center, you know. Been going there since my brother passed back in '98. He was a real good player, my brother. Always held onto his double six too long, though. I tell the fellas now, you can't hold onto it like that, but they don't listen. \n\nBut anyway, the men at the table... lately they've been giving me these little messages. Just little things meant for me. And it's not just them, sometimes the radio announcer does it too. I always listen to the AM station in the mornings while I have my coffee. Used to be a fellow on there who talked about the weather, sounded just like my Uncle Joe. Joe had a farm out east, grew the biggest tomatoes you ever saw... \n\nBut the messages, my wife, she says it's just talk. She tells me to just play my tiles and ignore it. But I know they are warning me to be careful. You just have to be careful these days. Especially with how they changed the bus schedule... I almost missed my ride here because they moved the stop down a whole block, didn't even put up a sign...\n\nDoctor: Do you believe for certain that the men at the domino table are sending you messages meant specifically for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, I know it for certain. Yes, sir. Even when my wife says it's just talk... she always says, \"Just play your tiles.\" She's been brushing me off like that since we bought our first house. Cost us eight thousand dollars back then. Eight thousand! And it had a real nice front porch. I used to sit out there in the evenings and whittle. Made a little wooden bird for my grandson once. He’s in college now, studying... well, I don't know what he's studying, something with the computers. Everything is computers now, nobody works with their hands. \n\nBut I know what I hear, doctor. Those men at the table, and the radio announcer too, they give little messages meant just for me. I know they are warning me to be careful. You just have to be careful. Like with the groceries... the prices they charge for a carton of eggs now, it's highway robbery. I went to the store on Tuesday and the girl at the register, she didn't even look up from her phone to give me my change. Just handed it right over without a word...\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory lately?\n\nPatient: Memory? No, doctor, my memory is sharp as a tack. I haven't noticed any trouble with it at all. Why, I can remember things from way back better than these young folks remember yesterday. Like my first car... it was a '54 Ford. Blue, with a white roof. I bought it off a fellow named Miller who lived down by the old creek. \n\nThat creek used to flood every spring, washed out the wooden bridge twice in one decade. They finally put in a concrete one, but it took the county three whole years to get around to it. Three years we had to drive the long way around past the Miller place just to get to town. He had these two big hound dogs, always howling at the moon... kept the whole neighborhood awake. Course, there wasn't much of a neighborhood back then, just dirt roads and pine trees. They cut all those trees down in the seventies to build that strip mall. The one with the hardware store that closed last year. Shame, really, they always had the best prices on nails...\n\nDoctor: Have there been times lately when you felt unusually confused or not fully clear-headed?\n\nPatient: Confused? No, no, my head's as clear as a bell. I don't get confused. Well, maybe just about how they run things down at city hall these days. You know, I went down there last month to pay my water bill. Used to be you could just hand it to Mary at the front desk. She's been there since... oh, must be 1985. We went to the same church, Saint Jude's, over on Elm Street. \n\nThey got a new priest there now, Father Thomas. Nice fellow, but he talks too fast. You can barely understand the homily. Not like Father O'Malley, he used to project his voice so even the folks in the back pew could hear every word. He had this big booming voice, like a foghorn. Speaking of fog, we used to get the thickest fog coming off the river in November. You couldn't even see your hand in front of your face. I remember one time, my buddy Frank and I were trying to walk home from the bowling alley in it... Frank, he bowled a 300 game once. Got his picture in the local paper and everything. They gave him a little trophy with a gold bowler on top...\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that should be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: Lost? Oh, no, sir. I know my way around this town like the back of my hand. I've walked these streets since they were dirt. Never get lost. Although, you know, they did go and change the name of Main Street to Martin Luther King Boulevard a few years back. Caused a whole lot of fuss at the time. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she was furious because she had to get all new return address labels printed. \n\nShe used to order those labels from a catalog... Harriet Carter, I think it was. They sold all sorts of little knick-knacks. I bought a set of those magnetic screwdrivers from them once. Best thing I ever bought. You drop a screw in the engine block, you just fish it right out. I was working on my son's lawnmower when I first used 'em. It was one of those old Snapper riding mowers, the red ones. He left it out in the rain all winter and the carburetor got all gummed up. Kids today, they don't take care of their things. Just throw it away and buy a new one, that's their motto. Like those paper plates... my wife bought a whole stack of them for the Fourth of July barbecue. We always had ribs, slow-cooked over hickory wood...\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you told you that your memory has been getting worse?\n\nPatient: Nobody's told me that. My daughter Betty comes by every Sunday and she's never said a peep about my memory. She usually brings a pot roast. Now, she makes a fair roast, but she puts way too much salt in the gravy. I always tell her she's gonna rust her pipes with all that salt, but she don't listen. Just like my brother Arthur, he used to salt his ham. Can you imagine that, salting a cured ham. He worked down at the railyard for forty years, loading freight. Hard work, that was. He had these big thick leather gloves he wore right through the fingers. Bought them at the old mercantile. You just can't find good leather gloves like that anymore. Everything now is made of plastic or nylon or whatever they call it. Makes your hands sweat something awful when the summer heat sets in...\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble managing your money or paying bills lately?\n\nPatient: Trouble with bills? No, no, I pay 'em just fine. Every month, right on time. I keep 'em all in this old cigar box on the kitchen table. It's a wooden box, real sturdy... Dutch Masters, I think. My uncle Charlie used to smoke those. He'd sit on the porch all evening, puffing away, telling stories about the Navy. He was stationed out in Hawaii before the war. Brought back this little ukulele for my sister. She tried to learn how to play it, but she didn't have much of an ear for music. Not like my cousin Sarah, she could play the piano by ear. Just hear a song on the radio once and sit down and play it perfect. We used to listen to the Grand Ole Opry on Saturday nights. Had this big wooden console radio, took up half the living room. The tubes in the back would glow orange when it warmed up...\n\nDoctor: Have you heard voices when no one else was there?\n\nPatient: Voices? Well, you know, sometimes in the afternoons when the house gets real quiet... I'll hear my old friend Jimmy call my name from out on the porch. Or sometimes he's humming one of those old choir songs, clear as day, like he's standing right there next to me. Jimmy had a beautiful baritone voice. We used to sing in the church choir together back in... oh, must have been the fifties. The choir director was a fellow named Mr. Henderson, wore these thick horn-rimmed glasses that always slid down his nose when he got worked up waving his arms around. He drove a Nash Ambassador, one of those big heavy cars that looked like an upside-down bathtub. Smooth ride, though. We took it all the way to the state fair one year, packed five of us in there with a picnic basket my mother made. She fried up a whole chicken in a cast iron skillet. You can't get chicken that tastes like that anymore, all the birds today are pumped full of water and raised in those big metal sheds...\n\nDoctor: Is Jimmy still alive?\n\nPatient: No, Jimmy... no, he passed away. It's been about a year and five months now since we lost him. That's right around when this all started, hearing him and all, right after he died. We laid him to rest out at Oak Hill Cemetery. They got him a real nice spot, right under this massive weeping willow tree. I always did like willow trees, the way they just sort of sway when the breeze catches them. My grandfather had a big old willow in his front yard back in Ohio. We used to swing from the branches when we were little. You gotta be careful, though, the wood on those branches is mighty soft. It snaps real easy if you put too much weight on it. I fell out of it once, oh, I must have been seven or eight, and scraped my knee up so bad my mother had to paint it with iodine. Boy, did that sting. It was bright orange, left a terrible stain on my good trousers. Getting stains out of clothes back then was a real chore, we didn't have these fancy washing machines with all the buttons and dials. Just a washboard and a big tin tub of hot soapy water...\n\nDoctor: Has your wife said that you’ve been more forgetful lately?\n\nPatient: Forgetful? No, Martha hasn't said anything to me about that. I remember things just fine. Now, Martha, she's the one who writes everything down on those little yellow pads. Leaves 'em stuck to the icebox door so she doesn't forget to buy milk or butter. We just got a new icebox last year, one of those shiny silver ones. Terrible thing to keep clean, shows every single fingerprint the second you touch it. I told her we should have kept the old white one, it ran just fine. It was a Whirlpool, bought it over at Sears Roebuck back when they had the big store downtown. You could buy anything at Sears back then, tools, clothes, even a whole house out of a catalog. My old neighbor, Mr. Higgins, his father built one of those catalog houses. Came on a train in a hundred different boxes, every piece of lumber stamped with a number. Took him and his brothers a whole summer to put it together. They used real solid oak for the floors, not like the cheap stuff they build with today. You walk into a new house now and the floors just squeak and bounce right under your boots...\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Depressed? No, I wouldn't say that. I feel alright, mostly. Just... you know, regular. Though the weather hasn't been helping much lately, has it? All this rain we've been getting, it just turns the yard into a muddy mess. Martha's been after me to reseed the back lawn, but you can't put down seed when the ground is soaking wet like a sponge. It just rots right there in the dirt. I used to keep a beautiful lawn, back when I had my old push mower. The heavy kind, with the spinning blades that you had to sharpen with a file. You don't see those much anymore. Now everyone's got these loud gas-powered things that sound like airplanes taking off on a Sunday morning. Wakes up the whole neighborhood. My neighbor two doors down, he starts his up right at seven o'clock. Guy works at the hardware store, sells paint, I think. Paint is so expensive nowadays, too. I went in there last month just looking for a little quart of white for the porch railing, and they wanted an arm and a leg for it...\n\nDoctor: What year is it right now?\n\nPatient: It's, uh... 2023, isn't it? Yes, 2023. I remember because Martha bought one of those big wall calendars with the pictures of the lighthouses on it for this year. She loves lighthouses. We took a trip up to Maine once, oh, it must have been thirty years ago now, just to drive up the coast and look at 'em. The water up there is so cold, though, even in the middle of summer. I dipped my toe in at a beach near Portland and thought my foot was going to freeze right off. We had some wonderful lobster on that trip, though. Bought it right off the boat from this fella in yellow overalls. You can't get seafood like that around here, it's all frozen and shipped in on trucks. By the time it gets to the grocery store, it doesn't even taste like fish anymore, just tastes like ice and cardboard. I tried to tell the butcher down at the Piggly Wiggly that once, but he just looked at me like I was crazy. Young kid, probably no older than nineteen, had his hat on backwards. I don't understand why kids today wear their hats backwards, the brim is supposed to keep the sun out of your eyes...\n\nDoctor: Has your wife ever had to take over things you used to handle on your own because you were having trouble doing them?\n\nPatient: Well, Martha hasn't exactly had to take over my chores, but I suppose I haven't been keeping up with my own things too well. I've let parts of my garden go lately, just weeds everywhere out by the back fence. I used to grow the biggest beefsteak tomatoes you ever saw. You slice one of those thick, put a little salt and some mayonnaise on white bread... oh, that's a meal right there. You can't get good white bread at the store anymore, it's all that expensive stuff with the hard seeds that get stuck under your dentures. My dentist, Dr. Evans, he's a nice enough fella but his hands are just too big for my mouth, always stretching my cheeks out. \n\nAnyway, I've also missed my choir rehearsals down at the church. Just haven't been going. We sing out of those old red hymnals, you know, the ones where the gold lettering is all rubbed off the covers from people holding them. I love the old hymns. My mother used to play them on the piano in our parlor when I was a boy. It was a big upright piano, heavy as a tractor. Took four men and a mule to move it into the house when my daddy bought it. \n\nAnd, well, I guess I made the domino games down at the lodge a bit uncomfortable for the fellas lately, too, so I just do not join like I used to. We always played on this green folding card table, but one of the legs was short, so somebody had to fold up a matchbook to stick under it so the tiles wouldn't slide. You don't see matchbooks much anymore. Every diner and gas station used to have a glass bowl of them right by the cash register, free for the taking, with their little pictures printed on the front. I used to keep a whole shoebox full of them in the garage...\n\nDoctor: Have you been seeing things that other people cannot see?\n\nPatient: Seeing things? No, no, my eyes aren't what they used to be, but I don't see anything that isn't there. Though... some afternoons, when the house is real quiet and Martha's out at the store, I do hear things. I hear my old friend Charlie calling my name right from the front porch. Sometimes he's humming one of those old choir songs we used to sing, clear as a bell, like he's standing right there next to the screen door. Charlie had a beautiful baritone voice, you know. Deep and rich, like molasses. We used to sit on that porch for hours, drinking iced tea out of those heavy glass mason jars. You don't see people using mason jars for drinking much anymore, mostly just for canning peaches or tomatoes. My grandmother used to can the best peaches, put 'em up in a sugary syrup that would stick to your fingers. We'd keep them down in the root cellar where it was nice and cool. Root cellars always smelled like damp earth and potatoes... potatoes are getting awfully small at the grocery store lately, have you noticed that? They used to be the size of softballs, and now they're just these little things, hardly worth peeling. Peeling potatoes is a young man's game anyway, my arthritis acts up if I hold the peeler too long...\n\nDoctor: Before these messages and voices started, had you been physically ill or had any major medical problem?\n\nPatient: Physically ill? No, I don't think so. I haven't had any big medical problems before all this started, just the usual aches and pains a fella my age gets. My knees click like a pair of castanets when I walk up the stairs, but that's just getting old, I suppose. Castanets... my sister Mary had a pair of those when she took up Spanish dancing back in the fifties. She wore this bright red dress with ruffles all down the skirt, twirling around in the living room until she knocked over Mother's good lamp. The one with the hand-painted roses on the shade. Mother was furious, of course. Sent Mary to her room without supper. Supper back then was always meat and potatoes, none of this fancy food you see on the television nowadays. People put avocado on toast now, can you believe that? Avocado is supposed to go in a salad, maybe with a little squeeze of lemon. Lemons used to be so cheap, you could buy a whole bag for a quarter down at the fruit stand on Elm Street... I wonder whatever happened to the man who ran that stand. He always wore a straw hat with a green ribbon...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

No clear preceding medical illness was identified, so F06.2 remains possible but less specifically supported by history alone; primary psychotic disorders and persistent delusional disorder remain in the differential.","is_final":false}

The tubes in the back would glow orange when it warmed up...\n\nDoctor: Have you heard voices when no one else was there?\n\nPatient: Voices? Well, you know, sometimes in the afternoons when the house gets real quiet... I'll hear my old friend Jimmy call my name from out on the porch. Or sometimes he's humming one of those old choir songs, clear as day, like he's standing right there next to me. Jimmy had a beautiful baritone voice. We used to sing in the church choir together back in... oh, must have been the fifties. The choir director was a fellow named Mr. Henderson, wore these thick horn-rimmed glasses that always slid down his nose when he got worked up waving his arms around. He drove a Nash Ambassador, one of those big heavy cars that looked like an upside-down bathtub. Smooth ride, though. We took it all the way to the state fair one year, packed five of us in there with a picnic basket my mother made. She fried up a whole chicken in a cast iron skillet. You can't get chicken that tastes like that anymore, all the birds today are pumped full of water and raised in those big metal sheds...\n\nDoctor: Is Jimmy still alive?\n\nPatient: No, Jimmy... no, he passed away. It's been about a year and five months now since we lost him. That's right around when this all started, hearing him and all, right after he died. We laid him to rest out at Oak Hill Cemetery. They got him a real nice spot, right under this massive weeping willow tree. I always did like willow trees, the way they just sort of sway when the breeze catches them. My grandfather had a big old willow in his front yard back in Ohio. We used to swing from the branches when we were little. You gotta be careful, though, the wood on those branches is mighty soft. It snaps real easy if you put too much weight on it. I fell out of it once, oh, I must have been seven or eight, and scraped my knee up so bad my mother had to paint it with iodine. Boy, did that sting. It was bright orange, left a terrible stain on my good trousers. Getting stains out of clothes back then was a real chore, we didn't have these fancy washing machines with all the buttons and dials. Just a washboard and a big tin tub of hot soapy water...\n\nDoctor: Has your wife said that you’ve been more forgetful lately?\n\nPatient: Forgetful? No, Martha hasn't said anything to me about that. I remember things just fine. Now, Martha, she's the one who writes everything down on those little yellow pads. Leaves 'em stuck to the icebox door so she doesn't forget to buy milk or butter. We just got a new icebox last year, one of those shiny silver ones. Terrible thing to keep clean, shows every single fingerprint the second you touch it. I told her we should have kept the old white one, it ran just fine. It was a Whirlpool, bought it over at Sears Roebuck back when they had the big store downtown. You could buy anything at Sears back then, tools, clothes, even a whole house out of a catalog. My old neighbor, Mr. Higgins, his father built one of those catalog houses. Came on a train in a hundred different boxes, every piece of lumber stamped with a number. Took him and his brothers a whole summer to put it together. They used real solid oak for the floors, not like the cheap stuff they build with today. You walk into a new house now and the floors just squeak and bounce right under your boots...\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Depressed? No, I wouldn't say that. I feel alright, mostly. Just... you know, regular. Though the weather hasn't been helping much lately, has it? All this rain we've been getting, it just turns the yard into a muddy mess. Martha's been after me to reseed the back lawn, but you can't put down seed when the ground is soaking wet like a sponge. It just rots right there in the dirt. I used to keep a beautiful lawn, back when I had my old push mower. The heavy kind, with the spinning blades that you had to sharpen with a file. You don't see those much anymore. Now everyone's got these loud gas-powered things that sound like airplanes taking off on a Sunday morning. Wakes up the whole neighborhood. My neighbor two doors down, he starts his up right at seven o'clock. Guy works at the hardware store, sells paint, I think. Paint is so expensive nowadays, too. I went in there last month just looking for a little quart of white for the porch railing, and they wanted an arm and a leg for it...\n\nDoctor: What year is it right now?\n\nPatient: It's, uh... 2023, isn't it? Yes, 2023. I remember because Martha bought one of those big wall calendars with the pictures of the lighthouses on it for this year. She loves lighthouses. We took a trip up to Maine once, oh, it must have been thirty years ago now, just to drive up the coast and look at 'em. The water up there is so cold, though, even in the middle of summer. I dipped my toe in at a beach near Portland and thought my foot was going to freeze right off. We had some wonderful lobster on that trip, though. Bought it right off the boat from this fella in yellow overalls. You can't get seafood like that around here, it's all frozen and shipped in on trucks. By the time it gets to the grocery store, it doesn't even taste like fish anymore, just tastes like ice and cardboard. I tried to tell the butcher down at the Piggly Wiggly that once, but he just looked at me like I was crazy. Young kid, probably no older than nineteen, had his hat on backwards. I don't understand why kids today wear their hats backwards, the brim is supposed to keep the sun out of your eyes...\n\nDoctor: Has your wife ever had to take over things you used to handle on your own because you were having trouble doing them?\n\nPatient: Well, Martha hasn't exactly had to take over my chores, but I suppose I haven't been keeping up with my own things too well. I've let parts of my garden go lately, just weeds everywhere out by the back fence. I used to grow the biggest beefsteak tomatoes you ever saw. You slice one of those thick, put a little salt and some mayonnaise on white bread... oh, that's a meal right there. You can't get good white bread at the store anymore, it's all that expensive stuff with the hard seeds that get stuck under your dentures. My dentist, Dr. Evans, he's a nice enough fella but his hands are just too big for my mouth, always stretching my cheeks out. \n\nAnyway, I've also missed my choir rehearsals down at the church. Just haven't been going. We sing out of those old red hymnals, you know, the ones where the gold lettering is all rubbed off the covers from people holding them. I love the old hymns. My mother used to play them on the piano in our parlor when I was a boy. It was a big upright piano, heavy as a tractor. Took four men and a mule to move it into the house when my daddy bought it. \n\nAnd, well, I guess I made the domino games down at the lodge a bit uncomfortable for the fellas lately, too, so I just do not join like I used to. We always played on this green folding card table, but one of the legs was short, so somebody had to fold up a matchbook to stick under it so the tiles wouldn't slide. You don't see matchbooks much anymore. Every diner and gas station used to have a glass bowl of them right by the cash register, free for the taking, with their little pictures printed on the front. I used to keep a whole shoebox full of them in the garage...\n\nDoctor: Have you been seeing things that other people cannot see?\n\nPatient: Seeing things? No, no, my eyes aren't what they used to be, but I don't see anything that isn't there. Though... some afternoons, when the house is real quiet and Martha's out at the store, I do hear things. I hear my old friend Charlie calling my name right from the front porch. Sometimes he's humming one of those old choir songs we used to sing, clear as a bell, like he's standing right there next to the screen door. Charlie had a beautiful baritone voice, you know. Deep and rich, like molasses. We used to sit on that porch for hours, drinking iced tea out of those heavy glass mason jars. You don't see people using mason jars for drinking much anymore, mostly just for canning peaches or tomatoes. My grandmother used to can the best peaches, put 'em up in a sugary syrup that would stick to your fingers. We'd keep them down in the root cellar where it was nice and cool. Root cellars always smelled like damp earth and potatoes... potatoes are getting awfully small at the grocery store lately, have you noticed that? They used to be the size of softballs, and now they're just these little things, hardly worth peeling. Peeling potatoes is a young man's game anyway, my arthritis acts up if I hold the peeler too long...\n\nDoctor: Before these messages and voices started, had you been physically ill or had any major medical problem?\n\nPatient: Physically ill? No, I don't think so. I haven't had any big medical problems before all this started, just the usual aches and pains a fella my age gets. My knees click like a pair of castanets when I walk up the stairs, but that's just getting old, I suppose. Castanets... my sister Mary had a pair of those when she took up Spanish dancing back in the fifties. She wore this bright red dress with ruffles all down the skirt, twirling around in the living room until she knocked over Mother's good lamp. The one with the hand-painted roses on the shade. Mother was furious, of course. Sent Mary to her room without supper. Supper back then was always meat and potatoes, none of this fancy food you see on the television nowadays. People put avocado on toast now, can you believe that? Avocado is supposed to go in a salad, maybe with a little squeeze of lemon. Lemons used to be so cheap, you could buy a whole bag for a quarter down at the fruit stand on Elm Street... I wonder whatever happened to the man who ran that stand. He always wore a straw hat with a green ribbon...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Confused? Well, Martha hasn't said anything like that, no. I don't think I've been confused. I mean, sometimes I misplace my glasses, but who doesn't? I usually leave them on the little table by the armchair. That armchair has been in the house since 1968. We bought it from Sears Roebuck. You used to be able to order just about anything from that Sears catalog... even a whole house! Can you imagine? Just ordering a house out of a book and having it delivered on a train. Trains used to run right through the center of town here, blew the whistle at noon every day. You could set your watch by it. My pocket watch belonged to my grandfather, actually. Silver case, heavy chain. He worked on the railroad, laid track all the way out to Omaha... Omaha's supposed to have good steaks, but I've never been. I don't travel much anymore, not since gas got so expensive. Used to be you could fill your tank for a few dollars and drive all Sunday afternoon just to look at the changing leaves...

Been going there since my brother passed back in '98. He was a real good player, my brother. Always held onto his double six too long, though. I tell the fellas now, you can't hold onto it like that, but they don't listen. \n\nBut anyway, the men at the table... lately they've been giving me these little messages. Just little things meant for me. And it's not just them, sometimes the radio announcer does it too. I always listen to the AM station in the mornings while I have my coffee. Used to be a fellow on there who talked about the weather, sounded just like my Uncle Joe. Joe had a farm out east, grew the biggest tomatoes you ever saw... \n\nBut the messages, my wife, she says it's just talk. She tells me to just play my tiles and ignore it. But I know they are warning me to be careful. You just have to be careful these days. Especially with how they changed the bus schedule... I almost missed my ride here because they moved the stop down a whole block, didn't even put up a sign...\n\nDoctor: Do you believe for certain that the men at the domino table are sending you messages meant specifically for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, I know it for certain. Yes, sir. Even when my wife says it's just talk... she always says, \"Just play your tiles.\" She's been brushing me off like that since we bought our first house. Cost us eight thousand dollars back then. Eight thousand! And it had a real nice front porch. I used to sit out there in the evenings and whittle. Made a little wooden bird for my grandson once. He’s in college now, studying... well, I don't know what he's studying, something with the computers. Everything is computers now, nobody works with their hands. \n\nBut I know what I hear, doctor. Those men at the table, and the radio announcer too, they give little messages meant just for me. I know they are warning me to be careful. You just have to be careful. Like with the groceries... the prices they charge for a carton of eggs now, it's highway robbery. I went to the store on Tuesday and the girl at the register, she didn't even look up from her phone to give me my change. Just handed it right over without a word...\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory lately?\n\nPatient: Memory? No, doctor, my memory is sharp as a tack. I haven't noticed any trouble with it at all. Why, I can remember things from way back better than these young folks remember yesterday. Like my first car... it was a '54 Ford. Blue, with a white roof. I bought it off a fellow named Miller who lived down by the old creek. \n\nThat creek used to flood every spring, washed out the wooden bridge twice in one decade. They finally put in a concrete one, but it took the county three whole years to get around to it. Three years we had to drive the long way around past the Miller place just to get to town. He had these two big hound dogs, always howling at the moon... kept the whole neighborhood awake. Course, there wasn't much of a neighborhood back then, just dirt roads and pine trees. They cut all those trees down in the seventies to build that strip mall. The one with the hardware store that closed last year. Shame, really, they always had the best prices on nails...\n\nDoctor: Have there been times lately when you felt unusually confused or not fully clear-headed?\n\nPatient: Confused? No, no, my head's as clear as a bell. I don't get confused. Well, maybe just about how they run things down at city hall these days. You know, I went down there last month to pay my water bill. Used to be you could just hand it to Mary at the front desk. She's been there since... oh, must be 1985. We went to the same church, Saint Jude's, over on Elm Street. \n\nThey got a new priest there now, Father Thomas. Nice fellow, but he talks too fast. You can barely understand the homily. Not like Father O'Malley, he used to project his voice so even the folks in the back pew could hear every word. He had this big booming voice, like a foghorn. Speaking of fog, we used to get the thickest fog coming off the river in November. You couldn't even see your hand in front of your face. I remember one time, my buddy Frank and I were trying to walk home from the bowling alley in it... Frank, he bowled a 300 game once. Got his picture in the local paper and everything. They gave him a little trophy with a gold bowler on top...\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that should be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: Lost? Oh, no, sir. I know my way around this town like the back of my hand. I've walked these streets since they were dirt. Never get lost. Although, you know, they did go and change the name of Main Street to Martin Luther King Boulevard a few years back. Caused a whole lot of fuss at the time. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she was furious because she had to get all new return address labels printed. \n\nShe used to order those labels from a catalog... Harriet Carter, I think it was. They sold all sorts of little knick-knacks. I bought a set of those magnetic screwdrivers from them once. Best thing I ever bought. You drop a screw in the engine block, you just fish it right out. I was working on my son's lawnmower when I first used 'em. It was one of those old Snapper riding mowers, the red ones. He left it out in the rain all winter and the carburetor got all gummed up. Kids today, they don't take care of their things. Just throw it away and buy a new one, that's their motto. Like those paper plates... my wife bought a whole stack of them for the Fourth of July barbecue. We always had ribs, slow-cooked over hickory wood...\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you told you that your memory has been getting worse?\n\nPatient: Nobody's told me that. My daughter Betty comes by every Sunday and she's never said a peep about my memory. She usually brings a pot roast. Now, she makes a fair roast, but she puts way too much salt in the gravy. I always tell her she's gonna rust her pipes with all that salt, but she don't listen. Just like my brother Arthur, he used to salt his ham. Can you imagine that, salting a cured ham. He worked down at the railyard for forty years, loading freight. Hard work, that was. He had these big thick leather gloves he wore right through the fingers. Bought them at the old mercantile. You just can't find good leather gloves like that anymore. Everything now is made of plastic or nylon or whatever they call it. Makes your hands sweat something awful when the summer heat sets in...\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble managing your money or paying bills lately?\n\nPatient: Trouble with bills? No, no, I pay 'em just fine. Every month, right on time. I keep 'em all in this old cigar box on the kitchen table. It's a wooden box, real sturdy... Dutch Masters, I think. My uncle Charlie used to smoke those. He'd sit on the porch all evening, puffing away, telling stories about the Navy. He was stationed out in Hawaii before the war. Brought back this little ukulele for my sister. She tried to learn how to play it, but she didn't have much of an ear for music. Not like my cousin Sarah, she could play the piano by ear. Just hear a song on the radio once and sit down and play it perfect. We used to listen to the Grand Ole Opry on Saturday nights. Had this big wooden console radio, took up half the living room. The tubes in the back would glow orange when it warmed up...\n\nDoctor: Have you heard voices when no one else was there?\n\nPatient: Voices? Well, you know, sometimes in the afternoons when the house gets real quiet... I'll hear my old friend Jimmy call my name from out on the porch. Or sometimes he's humming one of those old choir songs, clear as day, like he's standing right there next to me. Jimmy had a beautiful baritone voice. We used to sing in the church choir together back in... oh, must have been the fifties. The choir director was a fellow named Mr. Henderson, wore these thick horn-rimmed glasses that always slid down his nose when he got worked up waving his arms around. He drove a Nash Ambassador, one of those big heavy cars that looked like an upside-down bathtub. Smooth ride, though. We took it all the way to the state fair one year, packed five of us in there with a picnic basket my mother made. She fried up a whole chicken in a cast iron skillet. You can't get chicken that tastes like that anymore, all the birds today are pumped full of water and raised in those big metal sheds...\n\nDoctor: Is Jimmy still alive?\n\nPatient: No, Jimmy... no, he passed away. It's been about a year and five months now since we lost him. That's right around when this all started, hearing him and all, right after he died. We laid him to rest out at Oak Hill Cemetery. They got him a real nice spot, right under this massive weeping willow tree. I always did like willow trees, the way they just sort of sway when the breeze catches them. My grandfather had a big old willow in his front yard back in Ohio. We used to swing from the branches when we were little. You gotta be careful, though, the wood on those branches is mighty soft. It snaps real easy if you put too much weight on it. I fell out of it once, oh, I must have been seven or eight, and scraped my knee up so bad my mother had to paint it with iodine. Boy, did that sting. It was bright orange, left a terrible stain on my good trousers. Getting stains out of clothes back then was a real chore, we didn't have these fancy washing machines with all the buttons and dials. Just a washboard and a big tin tub of hot soapy water...\n\nDoctor: Has your wife said that you’ve been more forgetful lately?\n\nPatient: Forgetful? No, Martha hasn't said anything to me about that. I remember things just fine. Now, Martha, she's the one who writes everything down on those little yellow pads. Leaves 'em stuck to the icebox door so she doesn't forget to buy milk or butter. We just got a new icebox last year, one of those shiny silver ones. Terrible thing to keep clean, shows every single fingerprint the second you touch it. I told her we should have kept the old white one, it ran just fine. It was a Whirlpool, bought it over at Sears Roebuck back when they had the big store downtown. You could buy anything at Sears back then, tools, clothes, even a whole house out of a catalog. My old neighbor, Mr. Higgins, his father built one of those catalog houses. Came on a train in a hundred different boxes, every piece of lumber stamped with a number. Took him and his brothers a whole summer to put it together. They used real solid oak for the floors, not like the cheap stuff they build with today. You walk into a new house now and the floors just squeak and bounce right under your boots...\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Depressed? No, I wouldn't say that. I feel alright, mostly. Just... you know, regular. Though the weather hasn't been helping much lately, has it? All this rain we've been getting, it just turns the yard into a muddy mess. Martha's been after me to reseed the back lawn, but you can't put down seed when the ground is soaking wet like a sponge. It just rots right there in the dirt. I used to keep a beautiful lawn, back when I had my old push mower. The heavy kind, with the spinning blades that you had to sharpen with a file. You don't see those much anymore. Now everyone's got these loud gas-powered things that sound like airplanes taking off on a Sunday morning. Wakes up the whole neighborhood. My neighbor two doors down, he starts his up right at seven o'clock. Guy works at the hardware store, sells paint, I think. Paint is so expensive nowadays, too. I went in there last month just looking for a little quart of white for the porch railing, and they wanted an arm and a leg for it...\n\nDoctor: What year is it right now?\n\nPatient: It's, uh... 2023, isn't it? Yes, 2023. I remember because Martha bought one of those big wall calendars with the pictures of the lighthouses on it for this year. She loves lighthouses. We took a trip up to Maine once, oh, it must have been thirty years ago now, just to drive up the coast and look at 'em. The water up there is so cold, though, even in the middle of summer. I dipped my toe in at a beach near Portland and thought my foot was going to freeze right off. We had some wonderful lobster on that trip, though. Bought it right off the boat from this fella in yellow overalls. You can't get seafood like that around here, it's all frozen and shipped in on trucks. By the time it gets to the grocery store, it doesn't even taste like fish anymore, just tastes like ice and cardboard. I tried to tell the butcher down at the Piggly Wiggly that once, but he just looked at me like I was crazy. Young kid, probably no older than nineteen, had his hat on backwards. I don't understand why kids today wear their hats backwards, the brim is supposed to keep the sun out of your eyes...\n\nDoctor: Has your wife ever had to take over things you used to handle on your own because you were having trouble doing them?\n\nPatient: Well, Martha hasn't exactly had to take over my chores, but I suppose I haven't been keeping up with my own things too well. I've let parts of my garden go lately, just weeds everywhere out by the back fence. I used to grow the biggest beefsteak tomatoes you ever saw. You slice one of those thick, put a little salt and some mayonnaise on white bread... oh, that's a meal right there. You can't get good white bread at the store anymore, it's all that expensive stuff with the hard seeds that get stuck under your dentures. My dentist, Dr. Evans, he's a nice enough fella but his hands are just too big for my mouth, always stretching my cheeks out. \n\nAnyway, I've also missed my choir rehearsals down at the church. Just haven't been going. We sing out of those old red hymnals, you know, the ones where the gold lettering is all rubbed off the covers from people holding them. I love the old hymns. My mother used to play them on the piano in our parlor when I was a boy. It was a big upright piano, heavy as a tractor. Took four men and a mule to move it into the house when my daddy bought it. \n\nAnd, well, I guess I made the domino games down at the lodge a bit uncomfortable for the fellas lately, too, so I just do not join like I used to. We always played on this green folding card table, but one of the legs was short, so somebody had to fold up a matchbook to stick under it so the tiles wouldn't slide. You don't see matchbooks much anymore. Every diner and gas station used to have a glass bowl of them right by the cash register, free for the taking, with their little pictures printed on the front. I used to keep a whole shoebox full of them in the garage...\n\nDoctor: Have you been seeing things that other people cannot see?\n\nPatient: Seeing things? No, no, my eyes aren't what they used to be, but I don't see anything that isn't there. Though... some afternoons, when the house is real quiet and Martha's out at the store, I do hear things. I hear my old friend Charlie calling my name right from the front porch. Sometimes he's humming one of those old choir songs we used to sing, clear as a bell, like he's standing right there next to the screen door. Charlie had a beautiful baritone voice, you know. Deep and rich, like molasses. We used to sit on that porch for hours, drinking iced tea out of those heavy glass mason jars. You don't see people using mason jars for drinking much anymore, mostly just for canning peaches or tomatoes. My grandmother used to can the best peaches, put 'em up in a sugary syrup that would stick to your fingers. We'd keep them down in the root cellar where it was nice and cool. Root cellars always smelled like damp earth and potatoes... potatoes are getting awfully small at the grocery store lately, have you noticed that? They used to be the size of softballs, and now they're just these little things, hardly worth peeling. Peeling potatoes is a young man's game anyway, my arthritis acts up if I hold the peeler too long...\n\nDoctor: Before these messages and voices started, had you been physically ill or had any major medical problem?\n\nPatient: Physically ill? No, I don't think so. I haven't had any big medical problems before all this started, just the usual aches and pains a fella my age gets. My knees click like a pair of castanets when I walk up the stairs, but that's just getting old, I suppose. Castanets... my sister Mary had a pair of those when she took up Spanish dancing back in the fifties. She wore this bright red dress with ruffles all down the skirt, twirling around in the living room until she knocked over Mother's good lamp. The one with the hand-painted roses on the shade. Mother was furious, of course. Sent Mary to her room without supper. Supper back then was always meat and potatoes, none of this fancy food you see on the television nowadays. People put avocado on toast now, can you believe that? Avocado is supposed to go in a salad, maybe with a little squeeze of lemon. Lemons used to be so cheap, you could buy a whole bag for a quarter down at the fruit stand on Elm Street... I wonder whatever happened to the man who ran that stand. He always wore a straw hat with a green ribbon...\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you said that you’ve been more confused in your day-to-day life lately?\n\nPatient: Confused? Well, Martha hasn't said anything like that, no. I don't think I've been confused. I mean, sometimes I misplace my glasses, but who doesn't? I usually leave them on the little table by the armchair. That armchair has been in the house since 1968. We bought it from Sears Roebuck. You used to be able to order just about anything from that Sears catalog... even a whole house! Can you imagine? Just ordering a house out of a book and having it delivered on a train. Trains used to run right through the center of town here, blew the whistle at noon every day. You could set your watch by it. My pocket watch belonged to my grandfather, actually. Silver case, heavy chain. He worked on the railroad, laid track all the way out to Omaha... Omaha's supposed to have good steaks, but I've never been. I don't travel much anymore, not since gas got so expensive. Used to be you could fill your tank for a few dollars and drive all Sunday afternoon just to look at the changing leaves...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. We took a trip up to Maine once, oh, it must have been thirty years ago now, just to drive up the coast and look at 'em. The water up there is so cold, though, even in the middle of summer. I dipped my toe in at a beach near Portland and thought my foot was going to freeze right off. We had some wonderful lobster on that trip, though. Bought it right off the boat from this fella in yellow overalls. You can't get seafood like that around here, it's all frozen and shipped in on trucks. By the time it gets to the grocery store, it doesn't even taste like fish anymore, just tastes like ice and cardboard. I tried to tell the butcher down at the Piggly Wiggly that once, but he just looked at me like I was crazy. Young kid, probably no older than nineteen, had his hat on backwards. I don't understand why kids today wear their hats backwards, the brim is supposed to keep the sun out of your eyes...\n\nDoctor: Has your wife ever had to take over things you used to handle on your own because you were having trouble doing them?\n\nPatient: Well, Martha hasn't exactly had to take over my chores, but I suppose I haven't been keeping up with my own things too well. I've let parts of my garden go lately, just weeds everywhere out by the back fence. I used to grow the biggest beefsteak tomatoes you ever saw. You slice one of those thick, put a little salt and some mayonnaise on white bread... oh, that's a meal right there. You can't get good white bread at the store anymore, it's all that expensive stuff with the hard seeds that get stuck under your dentures. My dentist, Dr. Evans, he's a nice enough fella but his hands are just too big for my mouth, always stretching my cheeks out. \n\nAnyway, I've also missed my choir rehearsals down at the church. Just haven't been going. We sing out of those old red hymnals, you know, the ones where the gold lettering is all rubbed off the covers from people holding them. I love the old hymns. My mother used to play them on the piano in our parlor when I was a boy. It was a big upright piano, heavy as a tractor. Took four men and a mule to move it into the house when my daddy bought it. \n\nAnd, well, I guess I made the domino games down at the lodge a bit uncomfortable for the fellas lately, too, so I just do not join like I used to. We always played on this green folding card table, but one of the legs was short, so somebody had to fold up a matchbook to stick under it so the tiles wouldn't slide. You don't see matchbooks much anymore. Every diner and gas station used to have a glass bowl of them right by the cash register, free for the taking, with their little pictures printed on the front. I used to keep a whole shoebox full of them in the garage...\n\nDoctor: Have you been seeing things that other people cannot see?\n\nPatient: Seeing things? No, no, my eyes aren't what they used to be, but I don't see anything that isn't there. Though... some afternoons, when the house is real quiet and Martha's out at the store, I do hear things. I hear my old friend Charlie calling my name right from the front porch. Sometimes he's humming one of those old choir songs we used to sing, clear as a bell, like he's standing right there next to the screen door. Charlie had a beautiful baritone voice, you know. Deep and rich, like molasses. We used to sit on that porch for hours, drinking iced tea out of those heavy glass mason jars. You don't see people using mason jars for drinking much anymore, mostly just for canning peaches or tomatoes. My grandmother used to can the best peaches, put 'em up in a sugary syrup that would stick to your fingers. We'd keep them down in the root cellar where it was nice and cool. Root cellars always smelled like damp earth and potatoes... potatoes are getting awfully small at the grocery store lately, have you noticed that? They used to be the size of softballs, and now they're just these little things, hardly worth peeling. Peeling potatoes is a young man's game anyway, my arthritis acts up if I hold the peeler too long...\n\nDoctor: Before these messages and voices started, had you been physically ill or had any major medical problem?\n\nPatient: Physically ill? No, I don't think so. I haven't had any big medical problems before all this started, just the usual aches and pains a fella my age gets. My knees click like a pair of castanets when I walk up the stairs, but that's just getting old, I suppose. Castanets... my sister Mary had a pair of those when she took up Spanish dancing back in the fifties. She wore this bright red dress with ruffles all down the skirt, twirling around in the living room until she knocked over Mother's good lamp. The one with the hand-painted roses on the shade. Mother was furious, of course. Sent Mary to her room without supper. Supper back then was always meat and potatoes, none of this fancy food you see on the television nowadays. People put avocado on toast now, can you believe that? Avocado is supposed to go in a salad, maybe with a little squeeze of lemon. Lemons used to be so cheap, you could buy a whole bag for a quarter down at the fruit stand on Elm Street... I wonder whatever happened to the man who ran that stand. He always wore a straw hat with a green ribbon...\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you said that you’ve been more confused in your day-to-day life lately?\n\nPatient: Confused? Well, Martha hasn't said anything like that, no. I don't think I've been confused. I mean, sometimes I misplace my glasses, but who doesn't? I usually leave them on the little table by the armchair. That armchair has been in the house since 1968. We bought it from Sears Roebuck. You used to be able to order just about anything from that Sears catalog... even a whole house! Can you imagine? Just ordering a house out of a book and having it delivered on a train. Trains used to run right through the center of town here, blew the whistle at noon every day. You could set your watch by it. My pocket watch belonged to my grandfather, actually. Silver case, heavy chain. He worked on the railroad, laid track all the way out to Omaha... Omaha's supposed to have good steaks, but I've never been. I don't travel much anymore, not since gas got so expensive. Used to be you could fill your tank for a few dollars and drive all Sunday afternoon just to look at the changing leaves...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Harm me? Well... I don't know about harm exactly, but I do know I have to be careful. That's why I came in, really. The men down at the domino table at the senior center, they slip little messages into their talk. And the fellow on the radio, the one who does the evening news, he does it too. Little warnings, meant just for me. Martha tells me it's just regular talk, that I'm making a mountain out of a molehill, but I know what I hear. Dominoes is a funny game, you know. You have to match the dots... we used to play with these beautiful ivory tiles my uncle brought back from the war. He was in the Navy, stationed out in the Pacific. Sent home all sorts of things... a silk kimono for my mother, and those dominoes. They don't make them out of ivory anymore, of course. Banned it, I think. Probably for the best, you shouldn't go shooting elephants just for a board game. Elephants are smart animals, they say they never forget a thing. I saw an elephant at the circus when I was a boy, must have been 1948 or so. The whole tent smelled like roasted peanuts and sawdust. You could buy a bag of peanuts for a nickel back then... nickels used to have a buffalo on them, did you know that? I used to collect them in an old cigar box...

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF06.2, F03, F20.0, F22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor... I came in because... well, it's about the domino table. I play down at the center, you know. Been going there since my brother passed back in '98. He was a real good player, my brother. Always held onto his double six too long, though. I tell the fellas now, you can't hold onto it like that, but they don't listen. \n\nBut anyway, the men at the table... lately they've been giving me these little messages. Just little things meant for me. And it's not just them, sometimes the radio announcer does it too. I always listen to the AM station in the mornings while I have my coffee. Used to be a fellow on there who talked about the weather, sounded just like my Uncle Joe. Joe had a farm out east, grew the biggest tomatoes you ever saw... \n\nBut the messages, my wife, she says it's just talk. She tells me to just play my tiles and ignore it. But I know they are warning me to be careful. You just have to be careful these days. Especially with how they changed the bus schedule... I almost missed my ride here because they moved the stop down a whole block, didn't even put up a sign...\n\nDoctor: Do you believe for certain that the men at the domino table are sending you messages meant specifically for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, I know it for certain. Yes, sir. Even when my wife says it's just talk... she always says, \"Just play your tiles.\" She's been brushing me off like that since we bought our first house. Cost us eight thousand dollars back then. Eight thousand! And it had a real nice front porch. I used to sit out there in the evenings and whittle. Made a little wooden bird for my grandson once. He’s in college now, studying... well, I don't know what he's studying, something with the computers. Everything is computers now, nobody works with their hands. \n\nBut I know what I hear, doctor. Those men at the table, and the radio announcer too, they give little messages meant just for me. I know they are warning me to be careful. You just have to be careful. Like with the groceries... the prices they charge for a carton of eggs now, it's highway robbery. I went to the store on Tuesday and the girl at the register, she didn't even look up from her phone to give me my change. Just handed it right over without a word...\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory lately?\n\nPatient: Memory? No, doctor, my memory is sharp as a tack. I haven't noticed any trouble with it at all. Why, I can remember things from way back better than these young folks remember yesterday. Like my first car... it was a '54 Ford. Blue, with a white roof. I bought it off a fellow named Miller who lived down by the old creek. \n\nThat creek used to flood every spring, washed out the wooden bridge twice in one decade. They finally put in a concrete one, but it took the county three whole years to get around to it. Three years we had to drive the long way around past the Miller place just to get to town. He had these two big hound dogs, always howling at the moon... kept the whole neighborhood awake. Course, there wasn't much of a neighborhood back then, just dirt roads and pine trees. They cut all those trees down in the seventies to build that strip mall. The one with the hardware store that closed last year. Shame, really, they always had the best prices on nails...\n\nDoctor: Have there been times lately when you felt unusually confused or not fully clear-headed?\n\nPatient: Confused? No, no, my head's as clear as a bell. I don't get confused. Well, maybe just about how they run things down at city hall these days. You know, I went down there last month to pay my water bill. Used to be you could just hand it to Mary at the front desk. She's been there since... oh, must be 1985. We went to the same church, Saint Jude's, over on Elm Street. \n\nThey got a new priest there now, Father Thomas. Nice fellow, but he talks too fast. You can barely understand the homily. Not like Father O'Malley, he used to project his voice so even the folks in the back pew could hear every word. He had this big booming voice, like a foghorn. Speaking of fog, we used to get the thickest fog coming off the river in November. You couldn't even see your hand in front of your face. I remember one time, my buddy Frank and I were trying to walk home from the bowling alley in it... Frank, he bowled a 300 game once. Got his picture in the local paper and everything. They gave him a little trophy with a gold bowler on top...\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting lost in places that should be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: Lost? Oh, no, sir. I know my way around this town like the back of my hand. I've walked these streets since they were dirt. Never get lost. Although, you know, they did go and change the name of Main Street to Martin Luther King Boulevard a few years back. Caused a whole lot of fuss at the time. My neighbor, Mrs. Higgins, she was furious because she had to get all new return address labels printed. \n\nShe used to order those labels from a catalog... Harriet Carter, I think it was. They sold all sorts of little knick-knacks. I bought a set of those magnetic screwdrivers from them once. Best thing I ever bought. You drop a screw in the engine block, you just fish it right out. I was working on my son's lawnmower when I first used 'em. It was one of those old Snapper riding mowers, the red ones. He left it out in the rain all winter and the carburetor got all gummed up. Kids today, they don't take care of their things. Just throw it away and buy a new one, that's their motto. Like those paper plates... my wife bought a whole stack of them for the Fourth of July barbecue. We always had ribs, slow-cooked over hickory wood...\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you told you that your memory has been getting worse?\n\nPatient: Nobody's told me that. My daughter Betty comes by every Sunday and she's never said a peep about my memory. She usually brings a pot roast. Now, she makes a fair roast, but she puts way too much salt in the gravy. I always tell her she's gonna rust her pipes with all that salt, but she don't listen. Just like my brother Arthur, he used to salt his ham. Can you imagine that, salting a cured ham. He worked down at the railyard for forty years, loading freight. Hard work, that was. He had these big thick leather gloves he wore right through the fingers. Bought them at the old mercantile. You just can't find good leather gloves like that anymore. Everything now is made of plastic or nylon or whatever they call it. Makes your hands sweat something awful when the summer heat sets in...\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble managing your money or paying bills lately?\n\nPatient: Trouble with bills? No, no, I pay 'em just fine. Every month, right on time. I keep 'em all in this old cigar box on the kitchen table. It's a wooden box, real sturdy... Dutch Masters, I think. My uncle Charlie used to smoke those. He'd sit on the porch all evening, puffing away, telling stories about the Navy. He was stationed out in Hawaii before the war. Brought back this little ukulele for my sister. She tried to learn how to play it, but she didn't have much of an ear for music. Not like my cousin Sarah, she could play the piano by ear. Just hear a song on the radio once and sit down and play it perfect. We used to listen to the Grand Ole Opry on Saturday nights. Had this big wooden console radio, took up half the living room. The tubes in the back would glow orange when it warmed up...\n\nDoctor: Have you heard voices when no one else was there?\n\nPatient: Voices? Well, you know, sometimes in the afternoons when the house gets real quiet... I'll hear my old friend Jimmy call my name from out on the porch. Or sometimes he's humming one of those old choir songs, clear as day, like he's standing right there next to me. Jimmy had a beautiful baritone voice. We used to sing in the church choir together back in... oh, must have been the fifties. The choir director was a fellow named Mr. Henderson, wore these thick horn-rimmed glasses that always slid down his nose when he got worked up waving his arms around. He drove a Nash Ambassador, one of those big heavy cars that looked like an upside-down bathtub. Smooth ride, though. We took it all the way to the state fair one year, packed five of us in there with a picnic basket my mother made. She fried up a whole chicken in a cast iron skillet. You can't get chicken that tastes like that anymore, all the birds today are pumped full of water and raised in those big metal sheds...\n\nDoctor: Is Jimmy still alive?\n\nPatient: No, Jimmy... no, he passed away. It's been about a year and five months now since we lost him. That's right around when this all started, hearing him and all, right after he died. We laid him to rest out at Oak Hill Cemetery. They got him a real nice spot, right under this massive weeping willow tree. I always did like willow trees, the way they just sort of sway when the breeze catches them. My grandfather had a big old willow in his front yard back in Ohio. We used to swing from the branches when we were little. You gotta be careful, though, the wood on those branches is mighty soft. It snaps real easy if you put too much weight on it. I fell out of it once, oh, I must have been seven or eight, and scraped my knee up so bad my mother had to paint it with iodine. Boy, did that sting. It was bright orange, left a terrible stain on my good trousers. Getting stains out of clothes back then was a real chore, we didn't have these fancy washing machines with all the buttons and dials. Just a washboard and a big tin tub of hot soapy water...\n\nDoctor: Has your wife said that you’ve been more forgetful lately?\n\nPatient: Forgetful? No, Martha hasn't said anything to me about that. I remember things just fine. Now, Martha, she's the one who writes everything down on those little yellow pads. Leaves 'em stuck to the icebox door so she doesn't forget to buy milk or butter. We just got a new icebox last year, one of those shiny silver ones. Terrible thing to keep clean, shows every single fingerprint the second you touch it. I told her we should have kept the old white one, it ran just fine. It was a Whirlpool, bought it over at Sears Roebuck back when they had the big store downtown. You could buy anything at Sears back then, tools, clothes, even a whole house out of a catalog. My old neighbor, Mr. Higgins, his father built one of those catalog houses. Came on a train in a hundred different boxes, every piece of lumber stamped with a number. Took him and his brothers a whole summer to put it together. They used real solid oak for the floors, not like the cheap stuff they build with today. You walk into a new house now and the floors just squeak and bounce right under your boots...\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Depressed? No, I wouldn't say that. I feel alright, mostly. Just... you know, regular. Though the weather hasn't been helping much lately, has it? All this rain we've been getting, it just turns the yard into a muddy mess. Martha's been after me to reseed the back lawn, but you can't put down seed when the ground is soaking wet like a sponge. It just rots right there in the dirt. I used to keep a beautiful lawn, back when I had my old push mower. The heavy kind, with the spinning blades that you had to sharpen with a file. You don't see those much anymore. Now everyone's got these loud gas-powered things that sound like airplanes taking off on a Sunday morning. Wakes up the whole neighborhood. My neighbor two doors down, he starts his up right at seven o'clock. Guy works at the hardware store, sells paint, I think. Paint is so expensive nowadays, too. I went in there last month just looking for a little quart of white for the porch railing, and they wanted an arm and a leg for it...\n\nDoctor: What year is it right now?\n\nPatient: It's, uh... 2023, isn't it? Yes, 2023. I remember because Martha bought one of those big wall calendars with the pictures of the lighthouses on it for this year. She loves lighthouses. We took a trip up to Maine once, oh, it must have been thirty years ago now, just to drive up the coast and look at 'em. The water up there is so cold, though, even in the middle of summer. I dipped my toe in at a beach near Portland and thought my foot was going to freeze right off. We had some wonderful lobster on that trip, though. Bought it right off the boat from this fella in yellow overalls. You can't get seafood like that around here, it's all frozen and shipped in on trucks. By the time it gets to the grocery store, it doesn't even taste like fish anymore, just tastes like ice and cardboard. I tried to tell the butcher down at the Piggly Wiggly that once, but he just looked at me like I was crazy. Young kid, probably no older than nineteen, had his hat on backwards. I don't understand why kids today wear their hats backwards, the brim is supposed to keep the sun out of your eyes...\n\nDoctor: Has your wife ever had to take over things you used to handle on your own because you were having trouble doing them?\n\nPatient: Well, Martha hasn't exactly had to take over my chores, but I suppose I haven't been keeping up with my own things too well. I've let parts of my garden go lately, just weeds everywhere out by the back fence. I used to grow the biggest beefsteak tomatoes you ever saw. You slice one of those thick, put a little salt and some mayonnaise on white bread... oh, that's a meal right there. You can't get good white bread at the store anymore, it's all that expensive stuff with the hard seeds that get stuck under your dentures. My dentist, Dr. Evans, he's a nice enough fella but his hands are just too big for my mouth, always stretching my cheeks out. \n\nAnyway, I've also missed my choir rehearsals down at the church. Just haven't been going. We sing out of those old red hymnals, you know, the ones where the gold lettering is all rubbed off the covers from people holding them. I love the old hymns. My mother used to play them on the piano in our parlor when I was a boy. It was a big upright piano, heavy as a tractor. Took four men and a mule to move it into the house when my daddy bought it. \n\nAnd, well, I guess I made the domino games down at the lodge a bit uncomfortable for the fellas lately, too, so I just do not join like I used to. We always played on this green folding card table, but one of the legs was short, so somebody had to fold up a matchbook to stick under it so the tiles wouldn't slide. You don't see matchbooks much anymore. Every diner and gas station used to have a glass bowl of them right by the cash register, free for the taking, with their little pictures printed on the front. I used to keep a whole shoebox full of them in the garage...\n\nDoctor: Have you been seeing things that other people cannot see?\n\nPatient: Seeing things? No, no, my eyes aren't what they used to be, but I don't see anything that isn't there. Though... some afternoons, when the house is real quiet and Martha's out at the store, I do hear things. I hear my old friend Charlie calling my name right from the front porch. Sometimes he's humming one of those old choir songs we used to sing, clear as a bell, like he's standing right there next to the screen door. Charlie had a beautiful baritone voice, you know. Deep and rich, like molasses. We used to sit on that porch for hours, drinking iced tea out of those heavy glass mason jars. You don't see people using mason jars for drinking much anymore, mostly just for canning peaches or tomatoes. My grandmother used to can the best peaches, put 'em up in a sugary syrup that would stick to your fingers. We'd keep them down in the root cellar where it was nice and cool. Root cellars always smelled like damp earth and potatoes... potatoes are getting awfully small at the grocery store lately, have you noticed that? They used to be the size of softballs, and now they're just these little things, hardly worth peeling. Peeling potatoes is a young man's game anyway, my arthritis acts up if I hold the peeler too long...\n\nDoctor: Before these messages and voices started, had you been physically ill or had any major medical problem?\n\nPatient: Physically ill? No, I don't think so. I haven't had any big medical problems before all this started, just the usual aches and pains a fella my age gets. My knees click like a pair of castanets when I walk up the stairs, but that's just getting old, I suppose. Castanets... my sister Mary had a pair of those when she took up Spanish dancing back in the fifties. She wore this bright red dress with ruffles all down the skirt, twirling around in the living room until she knocked over Mother's good lamp. The one with the hand-painted roses on the shade. Mother was furious, of course. Sent Mary to her room without supper. Supper back then was always meat and potatoes, none of this fancy food you see on the television nowadays. People put avocado on toast now, can you believe that? Avocado is supposed to go in a salad, maybe with a little squeeze of lemon. Lemons used to be so cheap, you could buy a whole bag for a quarter down at the fruit stand on Elm Street... I wonder whatever happened to the man who ran that stand. He always wore a straw hat with a green ribbon...\n\nDoctor: Has anyone close to you said that you’ve been more confused in your day-to-day life lately?\n\nPatient: Confused? Well, Martha hasn't said anything like that, no. I don't think I've been confused. I mean, sometimes I misplace my glasses, but who doesn't? I usually leave them on the little table by the armchair. That armchair has been in the house since 1968. We bought it from Sears Roebuck. You used to be able to order just about anything from that Sears catalog... even a whole house! Can you imagine? Just ordering a house out of a book and having it delivered on a train. Trains used to run right through the center of town here, blew the whistle at noon every day. You could set your watch by it. My pocket watch belonged to my grandfather, actually. Silver case, heavy chain. He worked on the railroad, laid track all the way out to Omaha... Omaha's supposed to have good steaks, but I've never been. I don't travel much anymore, not since gas got so expensive. Used to be you could fill your tank for a few dollars and drive all Sunday afternoon just to look at the changing leaves...\n\nDoctor: Do you believe someone is trying to harm you?\n\nPatient: Harm me? Well... I don't know about harm exactly, but I do know I have to be careful. That's why I came in, really. The men down at the domino table at the senior center, they slip little messages into their talk. And the fellow on the radio, the one who does the evening news, he does it too. Little warnings, meant just for me. Martha tells me it's just regular talk, that I'm making a mountain out of a molehill, but I know what I hear. Dominoes is a funny game, you know. You have to match the dots... we used to play with these beautiful ivory tiles my uncle brought back from the war. He was in the Navy, stationed out in the Pacific. Sent home all sorts of things... a silk kimono for my mother, and those dominoes. They don't make them out of ivory anymore, of course. Banned it, I think. Probably for the best, you shouldn't go shooting elephants just for a board game. Elephants are smart animals, they say they never forget a thing. I saw an elephant at the circus when I was a boy, must have been 1948 or so. The whole tent smelled like roasted peanuts and sawdust. You could buy a bag of peanuts for a nickel back then... nickels used to have a buffalo on them, did you know that? I used to collect them in an old cigar box...\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
